SEC. 1639-1. G. C. GENERAL DEFINITIONS. 

The words "judge," "judge of the juvenile court," "juvenile judge" or "juvenile court," used in this chapter or under the laws of this state, shall be construed to mean the judge or the court exercising the powers and jurisdiction conferred in this chapter. 

The word "child" includes any child under eighteen years of age, except that wherever reference is made in this chapter to a crippled or otherwise physically handicapped child the word "child" shall include any person under twenty-one years of age. (a) The word "adult" includes any person eighteen years of age, or over, who is not a "child" within the meaning of this act. (b) The term "board" shall mean a county child welfare board. (c) The term "county department" shall mean a county department of welfare which has assumed the administration of child welfare. (d) The term "division" shall mean the division of social administration of the state department of public welfare. 44

(e) The term "foster home" shall mean a family home in which any child is received apart from its parents for care, supervision, or training, and the term "certified foster home" shall mean a foster home operated by a person or persons holding a permit in full force and effect issued pursuant to the statutes relating to certification. (f) The term "organization" shall mean any institution, public, semi-public or private, and any private association, society of agency located or operating in the state of Ohio, incorporated or unincorporated, having among its functions the furnishing of protective services or care for children, or the placement of children in foster homes or elsewhere; and the term "certified organization" shall mean any such organization holding a certificate in full force and effect, issued pursuant to statutes relating to the certification of child welfare organizations. (g) The term "relative" of, or person "related by blood or marriage" to any child shall mean any person or the spouse of any person whose relationship to such child is not more distant than that of first cousin; that is to say, not more distant than four degrees of relationship reckoned according to civil law.

The singular shall be construed to include the plural, the plural the singular, and the masculine the feminine, when consistent with the intent of this chapter.1 1. 

DISCUSSION: 

(a) History. Previous to the Nineteenth Century, all countries treated child offenders in the same manner as adults. This often resulted in placing children as young as 10 years in prisons with hardened criminals. In 1869 

Massachusetts passed a law making a distinction between children and adults. In 1899 Chicago established a department in its police court similar to the modern juvεnile court. Juvenile courts were first established on a state-wide basis in Ohio in 1904, although a juvenile court had been established in Cuyahoga County two years previously 

At the annual conference of the National Probation 

Association in Washington in May, 1923, a committee was appointed to prepare a standard Juvenile Court Law for adoption by the various states of the Union. This committee was composed of:

Franklin Chase Hoyt, Presiding Justice, Children's Court, New York, Chairman. 

Mary M. Bartelme, Judge, Juvenile Court, Chicago. 

Frederick P. Cabot, Judge, Juvenile Court, Boston. 

Bernard Flexner, Attorney-at-Law, New York. 

Charles W. Hoffman, Judge, Court of Domestic Relations, Cincinnati.

Sec. 1639-1 G. C., 121 O. L. 557. Effective Jan. 1, 1946. 

45

JUVENILE COURT 

Henry S. Hulbert, Judge, Probate Court, Detroit. 

James Hoge Ricks, Justice, Juvenile and Domestic

Relations Court, Richmond. 

Miriam Van Waters, Superintendent, State Reforma tory for Women, Framingham, Mass., formerly Referee, Juvenile Court, Los Angeles. 

Edward F. Waite, Judge, Juvenile Court, Minneapolis.

Charles L. Chute, Executive Director, National Proba tion Association, Secretary. 

This committee prepared a standard Juvenile Court

Law which has been revised from time to time. 

Prof. Felix Frankfurter of the Harvard Law School, now a justice of the United States Supreme Court, had this to say of the work of the committee: "You have rendered a real service in having formulated in your bill an agreement on the material furnished by the best experience in this country in the utilization of the juvenile court idea. The experience and disinterestedness of those who had a share in the drafting of this 

Act amply authenticates its importance."

At the annual convention of the Ohio Juvenile Judges' 

Association in January 1936, a committee was appointed to recodify the Ohio Code. The author had the honor of being chairman of this committee. The other members were: 

Hon. Harry L. Eastman, Juvenile Judge, Cleveland. 

Hon. Harry G. Gram, Juvenile Judge, Springfield. 

Hon. Sidney W. Jones, Juvenile Judge, Warren. 

Hon. V. A. Bennehoff, Juvenile Judge, Tiffin. 

Hon. Henry C. Ashcraft, Juvenile Judge, Newark. 

Hon. C. J. Anderson, Juvenile Judge, Mansfield. 

Hon. Homer W. Hammond, Juvenile Judge, Lisbon.

This committee adopted most of the proposals of the 

National Probation Association committee and presented a bill to the legislature which was passed and became effective on August 19, 1937. As a result, Ohio now has one of the best juvenile court laws of any state in the Union. Under it, the juvenile court has exclusive original jurisdiction of all delinquent, dependent and neglected children under 18 years of age, with a procedure free from unnecessary technicalities and with authority to establish adequate probation service, in proportion to the financial resources of th ecounty. (b) Purpose. The Ohio Juvenile Court Act has for its foundation the ancient doctrine of "parens patriae," under which the King's Chancellor of England intervened to protect neglected and dependent children. "The Court of Chancery," said Justice Nelson, in Williamson vs. 

Berry, 8 How. 555, "possesses an inherent jurisdiction which extends to the care of the persons of infants so far as is necessary for their protection and education, 46

and also to the care of their property. . . ." In applying the doctrine to delinquent children the modern juvenile court makes a new use of it; but in so doing is only extending to children up to the age of eighteen the common law rule that children under the age of seven are incapable of felonious intent.

Adopting these principles, the act prescribes throughout for cases of children, chancery or equity procedure rather than criminal. This is essential to give the court the scope of authority and discretion to deal adequately with each of the infinite variety of cases coming before it. 

It enables the court to deal with the child himself, and his condition, instead of merely with the particular circumstance which resulted in his being brought to court. 

In children's cases it dispenses with grand juries and indictments, with formal arraignments and pleas of guilty or not guilty; with the strict rules of evidence applicable in criminal trials, developed through generations to safeguard accused persons from tyranny, but often operating to obstruct a court endeavoring to befriend and correct a child; with public trials and juries; with criminal convictions, sentences and penalties. It deals with persons and situations, not with separate acts. The court has power to dispense with technicalities, consider social evidence, get the whole truth from all available sources, shield the child and the family from a publicity that may be destructive of their self-respect, and instead of punishment for the sake of punishment, substitute social treatment, though of course discipline for the child's own sake may be a useful element thereof. (c) Procedure under Ohio Law. Under the Ohio Act, children are dealt with separately from adults and their cases are heard at a different time and in a different place. They are tenanted in separate buildings, and if institutional guidance is necessary they are committed to institutions for children. Taking children from the parents is avoided when possible. Procedure is as informal as possible. The judge stands in the position of a friend of the children and bases his final disposition of the children on the psychology or temperament of the child, the character of its home, environment and of its parents. 

Reformation being the main object, punishment, as such, is seldom resorted to, and even when a commitment is made to an industrial school or reformatory it is not intended so much as punishment as it is to reclaim the 

Ichild to society.

The judge of a juvenile court should be well versed in law, and have a deep and abiding interest in the care and protection of children and in the improvement of his fellow-man. (d) Age. This section fixes the juvenile age for dependency, neglect and delinquency in both sexes as "un- 47

JUVENILE COURT 

der eighteen years of age." 

Eighteen years as the age limit for taking jurisdiction in delinquency cases is fixed by the juvenile court laws of most states. A higher limit is fixed in a few states while others have a different age for boys and girls. Seventeen years is adopted in some states. Sixteen years is the lowest age limit fixed in any state. With the safeguard for waiving jurisdiction provided in section 1639-32 G. C., for exceptional cases, eighteen years seems to be the practical limit for both sexes. There is no sound or well settled reason for the difference in ages now existing in the various states of the Union. 

(e) PRACTICE IN THE JUVENILE COURT By Harry L. Eastman 

Judge of the Juvenile Court of Cuyahoga County. 

The profession of law was one of the earliest to develop specialized fields of practice within itself, and to recognize that special knowledge and preparation were necessary for the successful practice of law before the several courts. Today the attorney either choose a certain field of specialization or he discovers that his opportunities draw him more frequently into a certain type of practice. He observes that each court, following its purpose and function, has a certain underlying philosophy and has developed a certain body of precedent and procedure. In time these become incorporated in the body of the law to which reference is made as the "Criminal 

Code," the "Probate Code," the "Bankruptcy Code," and others. The attorney must acquaint himself with all the codes in general, but more especially with the codes and methods of procedure of the courts to which his practice leads him.

While attorneys generally are familiar with the procedures and practices which have been developed in the Municipal, Criminal, Probate and Civil Courts and in the several State and Federal Courts, too few lawyers are familiar with the Ohio Juvenile Court Code and the practices and procedures in the Juvenile Court, nor do they fully apprehend the importance of the Court's place in the community.

The Origin of the Juvenile Court in Ohio. 

The need of a special court dealing with children was brought to the attention of the City of Cleveland by the late Newton D. Baker. In 1901, while serving as City 

Solicitor, he made an investigation of the conditions under which children were held in the city jails and tried in the police courts. He found the conditions shocking. In those days only slight concessions were made in favor of the youthful offender. Adolescent boys were confined in intimate contact with hardened malefactors. Young girls were held in the same quarters as prostitutes. 48

Mr. Baker communicated his findings to a group of progressive young lawyers and business men who were members of an active civic organization known as The Sociology Club. A committee was formed to effect some amelioration of the conditions Mr. Baker had described. 

The Chicago Juvenile Court, established in 1898, was studied by the Committee, which then sponsored a bill in the Ohio State Legislature establishing a similar Court for Cuyahoga County. This Court opened in June, 1902, as a part of Insolvency Court and became the second court of its kind in the United States.

Extension of the Juvenile Court Act. 

The first juvenile court act was rather simple and its application soon indicated where it needed amplification and strengthening. The first amendment was passed in 

1904. It authorized the employment of paid probation officers and provided for similar courts in eight additional counties of the state.

The first act had been weak in that, while it empowered the court to make orders concerning the care and disposition of neglected (now dependent) children, it provided no effective way of dealing with the adults who were responsible for the negect. This was partially remedied by an amendment in 1906 which permitted the court to assess fines and costs against adult contributors. 

The same amendment increased the number of paid probation officers permitted and made the appointment of at least one woman officer mandatory.

An amendment passed in 1908 raised the jurisdictional age from under 16 to under 17 years for both delinquents and dependents. Delinquent boys, 16 years old or over, who had committed felonies could be committed to the Ohio State Reformatory. Penalties against aduit con tributors were increased to include fines from $10.00 to $1000.00 and imprisonment up to one year, or both.

County Commissioners were authorized to provide detention homes, either by purchase or lease. 

An act passed in 1911 extended juvenile courts to seventeen counties of the state. It further defined the powers and duties of probation officers and provided that findings against a child in a juvenile court could not be used as evidence against that child in any other court. 

The act passed in 1913 made juvenile courts mandatory in every county in the state and raised the jurisdictional age for all children to under 18 years, where it has since remained. It more clearly defined the powers and limitations of the court and also the terms "deiinquent,” “dependent" and "proper parental care." It provided for physical and mental examinations of all children committed to institutions. In the same year the first Mothers' Pension law was enacted and the responsibility for its administration placed upon the Juvenile 49

JUVENILE COURT 

Courts. No major changes were made in the Juvenile 

Code until 1937, when the present revision, sponsored by the Ohio State Association of Juvenile Court Judges, was enacted.

The Common Law Basis of Juvenile Court Legislation. 

While the Court itself is of comparatively recent origin, the legal principles underlying it stem from the 

English common law of chancery. Blackstone tells us that the King, in his capacity parens patriae, assumed the general protection of all infants in his kingdom, through the keeper of his conscience, his Chancellor. 

The general acceptance of the doctrine investing courts of chancery with equity jurisdiction over the persons and estates of minors was fully set forth by Lord Jeky.l in 

1772 and has been unanimously adopted by his successors. Its application to the Juvenile Court has been set forth by Flexner and Oppenheimer as follows: 

"That the Juvenile Court is of chancery origin has beɛn *** affirmed by the overwhelming opinion of courts of last resort. It has generally been maintained that the Juvenile Court is but the embodiment in the law and in a specific institution of an ancient doctrine and of modern methods in the exercise of the power of the state as the ultimate parent of the child."

Shortly after the institution of the Court the statutes were amended to give it jurisdiction over neglected and dependent children (1906) and over adults who contributed to or acted in a way tending to cause these conditions. These powers were extended by amendments in 

1911 and further defined and amplified by the Juvenile 

Code passed in 1912.

Delinquent Children. 

The child offender is brought before the Court, not as a felon or misdemeanant, but as a "delinquent." By this term the law distinguishes between the guilty intent of the adult and the incapacity of the child and extends such incapacity from the under seven years of the common law to the under eighteen years in the juvenile code. 

While the juvenile may not be punished under the criminal law, the statutes recognize his need for correction and discipline and make provision for it. 

A delinquent child is defined in G. C. 1639-2 as anyone under eighteen years of age who violates any law or ordinance or who is wayward or habitually disobedient; or who is a habitual runaway from home or truant from school; or who behaves in such a manner as to endanger the health or morals of itself or others; or who attempts to enter the marriage relation contrary to the law of this state.

The Juvenile Court is given exclusive original jurisdiction over all child offenders, Sec. 1639-29, and if the 50

child is brought before another Court, such Court must immediately transfer the case to the Juvenile Court. 

However, it should always be borne in mind that the Juvenile Code Sec. 1639-1 defines a child as one under eighteen years of age and an adult as one eighteen years of age or over. This is quite different from the usual conception that a child becomes an adult only when he reaches his majority.

The code recognizes that certain mature children may commit acts of such a serious nature and be so habituated to anti-social attitudes that the remedies at the disposal of the Juvenile Court may be inadequate to bring about his reformation and to protect the rights of society. It therefore provides, Sec. 1639-32, that when a juvenile has committed a felonious offense, the Juvenile Court may, after a careful physical, psychological and psychiatric examination, waive its jurisdiction and bind the juvenile over to the Grand Jury for indictment and trial under criminal procedure. Except as so bound over, a juvenile may not be charged, convicted or sentenced as a criminal in the State of Ohio. 

For his further protection, Sec. 1639-30 provides that no adjudication of a delinquent by a Juvenile Court shall be construed as a conviction and no order of the Court as a sentence, nor shal. the child be considered a criminal by reason of such order nor shall it operate to impose any of the civil disabilities ordinarily consequent upon a conviction. Furthermore, the same section provides that neither the disposition ordered by the Court nor any evidence given in the court shall be admissible as evidence against the child in any case or proceedings in any other court, nor shall it operate to disqualify the child from any future civil service examination, appointment or application. * * The purpose and construction of the Ohio Code is set forth in Sec. 1639-59 as follows: "The purpose of this chapter is to secure for each child under its jurisdiction such care, guidance and control * as will serve the child's best welfare and the best interests of the state." 

The principle is thereby recognized that children under the jurisdiction of the court are wards of the state, subject to the discipline and entitled to the protection of the state which may intervene to safeguard them from neglect or injury and to enforce the legal obligations due to them and from them. 

Informality of Procedure.

Since the purpose of the Court in dealing with chidren is corrective and not punitive, it has been provided by the statutes that the hearings shall be as informal as possible. (G. C. Sec. 1639-30.)

In the hearing of a delinquency case, all those having 51

JUVENILE COURT 

an interest in the case are permitted to be present. The complainant states his case and the circumstances surrounding it. If there is any dispute about the facts, the child is asked to tell his version of the occurrence, or the probation officer may state the facts for him. If necessary, witnesses are heard to substantiate the statements of either. If counsel is present, he may examine both or either party. The Judge may also ask questions designed to bring out the full facts. After the delinquency is established, the Judge refers to the investigation report of the probation officer.

The Right to Counsel. 

The right to counsel in all cases brought before the Juvenile Court is fully recognized. However, it is infrequent that attorneys appear in delinquency cases and then generally it is to represent adults charged with contributing to the delinquency of a minor. When an attorney does appear in the interest of a child, he finds that the concern of the Court is the welfare of his client and upon this common ground cooperation is easily established. Because the attorney a.ready has the confidence of the family he can assist the Court in many ways in working out a satisfactory plan for the child. 

Disposition of Children. 

The essential consideration in the disposition of cases and the treatment that follows is the welfare of the child. Before that can be determined the Court must learn all that can be discovered about the child as an individual, about his home and environment and about the influences that have brought about his present predicament. The assembling of these facts prior to the hearing is a part of the duties of the probation officers of the Court outlined in G. C. Sec. 1639-19, and constitutes the "investigation report" to which reference has been made.

This contains such social facts as family background; parental interest and control; school placement and adjustment; recreational interests and outlets; social adjustment and pertinent facts concerning the neighborhood and environment. The possible bearing of these factors upon the conduct of the child is discussed with the parents or family representative and with the probation officer and the representatives of any interested agencies. From these discussions a plan of social treatment for the child is made and the order of the Court entered in conformity with such plan.

This written report is available to the Court. Attached to it is usually a report on the physical condition of the child following an examination made by the court physician and also the report of the psychologist. In addition, in cases presenting serious problems of behavior, a report from the psychiatrist is included. Authority to require 52

such examinations of all persons brought before the 

Court is given in G. C. Sec. 1639-54. 

The Court is given wide discretion (G. C. Sec. 1639-30) 

in the disposition of the children that come before it. 

This wide discretion is necessary if the Court is to best serve the interests of the great variety of delinquent, dependent and neglected children whose problems it is expected to solve. The majority of cases are summarily adjusted.

Commitments to correctional school are made only after careful study has shown that such disposition is for the best interest of the child and society. 

The majority of children who are neglected by reason of non-support are returned to their homes. A number of these cases concern children of divorced families and reach the Court through transfer of jurisdiction from the Common Pleas Court in which the decree was granted. 

The majority of dependent children are committed to child caring agencies as such complaints are seldom brought into court until such an order becomes necesary. The Court is extremely reluctant to break up families and does not do so until every other method of adjustment has been exhausted. Commitments to child caring agencies are for temporary care and the chiid is returned to his parents if possible when it is demonstrated that the objectionable conditions that caused his removal have been corrected or removed.

If, after a period of such care, it is found impossible to rehabilitate the child's own home or to locate his parents, the Court may then make an order committing the child to the agency for permanent care or adoption. If adoption is sought, proceedings must be had in the Probate Court where the agency has authority to consent to the petition. The Juvenile Court has no authority to grant adoptions.

Jurisdiction in Adult Cases. 

The Juvenile Court also exercises criminal jurisdiction over adults charged with contributing to the neglect, dependency or delinquency of a juvenile, offenses against or involving children and the determination of paternity. 

It is in this type of case that attorneys most frequently appear in this court. The hearings are separate from those for juveniles and the procedure conforms to that of the Common Pleas Court.

Juvenile Courts in Ohio have long had jurisdiction in bastardy cases concurrent with the Courts of Common Pleas.

The Use of Social Case Histories. 

The use of social histories, of physical, psychological and psychiatric examination, first developed in the juvenile courts, has now been adopted, at least to some extent, in various municipal, county and federal criminal 53

JUVENILE COURT 

courts. While in some courts they are not made until after trial and convictions, in some instances, such as under the Briggs law of Massachusetts, their application is routine. At least, the judge has social records before him before he imposes sentence.

The contributions of the social sciences to law are too frequently underestimated, especially among lawyers. 

Even where laws have been liberalized and the law of reta.iation replaced by sincere attempts to accomplish the reformation and restoration of the offender to useful citizenship, the part that social work bears in the accomplishment of this end is not always clearly understood or fully appreciated. Law and social work have come to supplement each other in the solution of those social problems for which neither field alone has adequate equipment.

The Juvenile Court, although it uses some of the methods and procedures of social work, still remains a court of law. Its use in its probation department of the practices and procedures of social work is justified by the fact that its work concerns immature children and that its objectives are educational and correctional rather than punitive.

Practice in Juvenile Court. 

In the early days of the court, when hearings were almost exclusively concerned with child delinquents under sixteen years of age, a belief developed that defense council was not necessary. This notion was encouraged by members of the profession who felt that the purpose of the court was protective rather that punitive. This conception persisted even after the age jurisdiction was raised to under eighteen years and jurisdiction over adult contributors was widened. It seems that there are some lawyers who still hold the mistaken belief that they are unwelcome in the Juvenile Court.

The practice of attorneys in the Juvenile Court has always been welcomed. Staff members are instructed to show every courtesy to lawyers and to facilitate their work whenever possible. Preference is always given to attorneys in arranging the daily order of cases so as to avoid unnecessary waiting or loss of time on their part.

Their constructive criticism in any particular has always been welcomed.

The Court assumes that every attorney is equally interested in solving the problems presented by his particular case for the best interest of the child concerned, rather than for the selfish interest of an erring parent or other adults; that his duty to his client ends where it jeopardizes or conflicts with the welfare of the childand the community. That being so, and the attorney having the confidence of his client, it is apparent that his aid and assistance is most valuable to the Court in 54

producing the true facts and in influencing his client, through explanation and persuasion, to cooperate with the Court in order to serve his own best interests. 

It is hoped that members of the Bar will visit their Juvenile Court and directly acquaint themselves with its procedure and personnel. The better understanding thus obtained will assure them of their welcome and convince them that the Court occupies a necessary place in the community, that its informal judicial atmosphere is best adapted to carrying out its social functions, and that it deserves their fullest cooperation and support. (f) 

Synopsis of THE OHIO JUVENILE COURT CODE OF 1937 by Paul W. Alexander Judge of Juvenile Court, Lucas County Part I SOCIAL PHILOSOPHY OF THE LAW AS EXPRESSED IN THE NEW CODE 

Child's Best Welfare; State's Interest; Home Care.

Purpose is to secure for each child such care, guidance and control, preferably in its own home, as will serve the child's best welfare, and the best interests of the state. 

When child is removed from its own family it is intended to secure for it custody, care and discipline as similar as possible to that which should have been given by its parents. Children under jurisdiction of court are wards of the state, subject to the discipline and entitled to protection of state which may intervene to safeguard them from neglect or injury, and to enforce legal obligations due to and from them. To this end the code must be liberally construed. (59) 

Criminal Charge Forbidden Again Child; Exception. 

No child shall be charged with or convicted of a crime in any court-not even in juvenile court! Single exception: where act would be felony if committed by adult, juvenile judge may, after investigation and mental and physical examination, bind child over to Common Pleas Court. (30, 32) Cooperation of Entire Government Mandatory; Especially Officers. Every county, city and township official and department has duty to render all assistance and cooperation in its power to help carry out purpose of code. 

Court has specific authority to seek cooperation of child welfare agencies, etc. (55). All sheriffs and deputies, police chiefs and officers, constables, marshals, etc., shall, upon request, render assistance to probation officers in performance of their duties (19).

Child Must Not Be Taken To Police Station; All Other 

Courts Barred. No child shall be placed in any prison, jail or lock-up; nor brought into any police station; nor any vehicle (patrol wagon) with adult prisoners. into 55

JUVENILE COURT 

Exception: judge or chief probation officer may have child held in separate quarters in jail, if over 14. (22)

Child arrested, even for feiony, shall be taken directly before juvenile judge. No other court may entertain case for an instant. When child is delivered to juvenile court all further proceedings in other court are discontinued (29). 

No Interference By Habeas Corpus. When child has been committed to party by juvenile court, no other court shall issue a writ of habeas corpus without first hearing such party, or until his right to hold child has been finally determined by proper court. (35)

Hearings Private & Informal; No Jury. Children's cases must be heard in separate room, apart from criminal court. (14, 30) Hearing may be informally conducted; general public may be excluded; no jury in children's cases. (30)

Records Privileged and Confidential. Reports and rec ords of probation department are confidential and shall not be made public. (19) Disposition of a child or evidence given in juvenile court shall not be admissible as evidence against the child in any other court. (30). Records shall be open only by order of court to persons having legitimate interest. (13)

Scientific Approach Authorized. Physicians, psycholo gists and psychiatrists may be appointed to examine child or adult coming before court, and county shall bear expense thereof. (54) County commissioners authorized to provide adequate facilities to be used as laboratories, dispensaries or clinics for use of scientific specialists connected with the court. (15)

Foster Home Subsidy. To have foster homes available at all time an agreed monthly subsidy may be paid by county.(22) Detention home to resemble private home. (22) Religious Faith. In separating child from parents court shall, when practicable, place child with person or agency of same religious faith as (first) the parents; (second) the child itself; (third) either parent. (33)

Appropriation Mandatory. County commissioners must appropriate enough to meet all administrative expenses of juvenile court, plus child care (foster homes, agencies, institutions, etc.) (57)

Part II DEFINITION OF TERMS 

Child. Anyone under 18. (1) Intervention of 18th birthday between act complained of and complaint or hearing does not deprive court of jurisdiction. (30) 

Adult. Anyone 18 or over. (1)

Delinquent Child. One who: 

1. Violates any law. 

2. Is uncontrollable, wayward or habitually disobedient. 56

3. Is habitual truant (from home or school). 

4. Endangers morals or health. 

5. Marries without parental consent (even outside 

Ohio). (2)

Neglected Child. One who: 

1. Is abandoned. 

2. Lacks parental care through parental fault. 

3. Is refused necessaries or education by parents, etc. 

4. Is refused special care (if feeble-minded) by parents, etc. 

5. Is found in bad company, etc. 

6. Works at forbidden employment. (3) Dependent Child. One who: 

1. Lacks parental care without parental fault. 

2. Lacks parental care, parents being sick or feebleminded. 

3. Has condition or environment that warrants state in assuming guardianship, in interests of child. (4) 

Parental Care. Is lacking when: 

1. Home is unsanitary. 

2. Child is permitted to become dependent, neglected or delinquent. 

3. Able parents refuse necessaries or education. 

4. Parents fail to discipline child. (5) 

Residence. Residence of child is that of parents when parents are together; when parents have different residences, residence of child is that of legal guardian or person in loco parentis. (6)

Part III ORGANIZATION OF COURT Court and Judge. Court of Common Pleas, Division of Domestic Relations, shall exercise powers and jurisdic tion conferred by Juvenile Court Code in the seven most populous counties, including Lucas. Cuyahoga is sui juris. 

In other 80 counties the juvenile court is created within the probate court. When judge is away, common pleas judge may be assigned to carry on (7, 8). 

Room and Building. Commissioners provide special room (14) or separate building appropriately constructed and furnished for business of court and all parts thereof and its employees (15). 

Staff. Judge may appoint chief probation officer and as many probation officers and other employees as necessary. These constitute the probation department. Judge fixes salaries. May require bond of employees (18). 

County Probation. Where county probation department exists, and no juvenile court probation officer is appointed, county department shall serve as probation officer (20).

Referees. Judge may appoint referees having usual 57

JUVENILE COURT 

powers of masters in chancery cases. (After notice to parties of referee's findings and recommendations, judge may adopt same, hear additional testimony, modify or set aside). Female referee mandatory for females (21).

Clerk. In the seven domestic relations counties clerk of common pleas court shall keep the records. In all others judge is clerk of his own court (17).

Detention Home; Staff. Upon advice of judge, commissioners provide Detention Home, within convenient distance of court, for detention of children pending final disposition of their cases. Judge appoints staff, consisting of superintendent, matron and other necessary employees; judge fixes salaries (22).

Part IV OPERATION OF COURT 

CHILDREN'S CASES Jurisdiction in Children's Cases. Exclusive and original jurisdiction vests in juvenile court: 

1. Concerning delinquent, neglected, dependent or crippled child. 

2. To determine custody of any child (except ward of some other court). 

3. To determine bastardy cases (usually adult). 

4. To provide for custody, care and support in cases certified to it after divorce granted (16). 

Duties of Probation Officers. Chief probation officer has charge. Probation officers shail: 

Make investigations. 

Keep records of investigations.

Furnish probationer statement of conditions of probation. 

Keep informed concerning probationers, etc. 

Improve probationers' conduct and condition.

Keep full records of work done. 

Keep accounts of money collected (19).

Transmit summary of information when child is committed (30).

Powers of Probation Officers. Probation officer has power to: 

Serve warrants, etc., anywhere in Ohio. 

Arrest without warrant;* (a) Upon reasonable information. (b) Upon view. 

Detain accused while obtaining warrant. 

Call on police, sheriff, etc., for help (19).

Complaints. Any person having knowledge of child appearing to be delinquent, neglected, dependent or crippled, may file sworn complaint. Complaint sufficiently definite if it simply uses words "delinquent," etc. (23). *Probably limited to violations of laws relating to children. 58

Citations.** Following complaint and investigation, court issues citation ordering parent or custodian to appear and bring child.

Parents must always be notified of hearing. 

Child may be brought in by probation officer if order is endorsed on citation.

Failure to obey citation or subpoena is contempt of court (24).

Service of Process. On residents of Ohio: 

Citations, notices and subpoenas may be served personally or, on order of judge, by registered mail (25). 

Personal Service. Writ may issue to and be served by: 

Probation officer of this court. 

Sheriff, any county. 

Any marshal. 

Any constable. 

Any police officer (52). 

Warrant vs. parent or child to produce child at once may be issued (26).

Service by Publication. On non-resident of Ohio: 

1. Affidavit is filed that parent, custodian, etc., is outside Ohio, or residence unknown, so citation cannot be served. 

2. Clerk publishes, one insertion, in one newspaper of general circulation, the citation, which shall state: 

The substance (sic). 

The time and place of hearing. 

3. Clerk mails copy of citation to last known address, unless affidavit is filed showing address unobtainable. 

4. One week must elapse from date of publication to hearing.* 

5. Clerk certifies publication or (sic) mailing of citation (25). 

Release to Parent. Officer taking child must, unless impracticable, or ordered otherwise, release child to parent on latter's written promise to produce child when ordered.

Foregoing does not prevent officer from picking up child breaking the law, running away, or in bad surroundings (27). 

Temporary Detention. When not released to parent, child is detained wherever court designates (27, 28). **Apparently a "citation" is an order to actual custodian of child; "notice" is simply to inform parent who is not custodian, of the hearing and he may appear or not, as he pleases; "subpoena" is an order to anyone to appear and testify as witness; "warrant" is order to officer to make an arrest; "summons" is order to party to civil action to appear or answer; "writ" covers all of foregoing, and many more. *No time fixed to elapse between personal service and hearing.

Revenue from Imprisonment. County must appropri ate and pay 50c per day for children of party imprisoned for neglect (47). 

Appeals.* Appeals on questions of law may be taken from prosecutions of adults; no appeal unless, after notice to prosecuting attorney, motion is filed and heard showing good cause for appeal-or such motion is allowed by Court of Appeals (51). *The juvenile court code makes no provision for appeal of children's cases.

Part V MISCELLANEOUS PROVISIONS 

Support-Money. Court may order parent, etc., to pay for support of child, etc. (34).

Judgment, Execution. Court may enter judgment for amount due and levy execution therefor* (34). 

Certify Non-Resident. If child is non-resident case may be certified to county of child's residence (34). 

From Other State. Under penalty ($300, 3 mos.) no child may be placed in Ohio from another state unless guaranty be furnished to state department of public welfare. (1639-37, 12789-1 G. C.) 

Return, Other State. Child who is non-resident of state may be committed to Ohio department of public welfare for return to home state (34). 

Charge Other County. Non-resident of county may be kept in county and his care charged to county of residence, with consent of other judge (34).

County Pays All. Any expense created by court for child, except state or federal obligations, shall be paid from county treasury (34).

Costs, Child Cases. No costs shall accrue in children's cases (unless child is fined the costs) (56).

Adult Cases. In adult cases costs are computed the same as in common pleas court (56). 

Transport Expense. Expense of transporting children shall be paid out of county treasury (56). 

Service Costs. Cost of service of writs issued to other *This seems to authorize juvenile courts to render "lump sum judgments." 62

officers shall be paid by county, city or township, as case may be, in the usual manner (53).

Seal. Seal of court shall be attached to all writs and processes (9). 

Contempt. Court has same jurisdiction in contempt as common pleas (10). 

Rules. Rules of procedure for officers and employees may be framed and published** (11). 

Place of Holding. Court may be held anywhere in county (12). 

Pending Cases. Pending cases carried over Aug. 19, 1937, and from one term to next (12, 58).

Agency Reports. Judge may require reports from any agency desiring to accept children (36).

Independent Sections. Sections of Act independent 

(60).

Name of Act. Name, "Juvenile Court Code" (61). 

**An attorney being "an officer of the court" is bound by the rules of the court.

SEC. 1639-2 G. C. DELINQUENT CHILD DEFINED. 

For the purpose of this chapter, the words "delinquent child" includes any child: 

1. Who violates any law of this state, the United States, or any ordinance or regulation of a subdivision of the state. 

2. Who does not subject itself to the reasonable control of its parents, teachers, guardian or custodian, by reason of being wayward or habitually disobedient. 

3. Who is an habitual truant from home or school. 

4. Who so deports itself as to injure or endanger the morals or health of itself or others. 

5. Who attempts to enter the marriage relation in this or any other state without the consent of its parents, custodian, legal guardian or other legal authority, as required by the laws of this state.2

DISCUSSION: 

With the exception of sub-section 5 above, this section is a most identical with the Uniform Act. 

Such delinquency is not considered a crime.3 An act cannot be considered delinquent unless it is within the terms of the statute.4 Knowingly visiting a house of ill repute,5 making a false affidavit to secure a marriage license unlawfully,6 playing pool in a public pool room,7 

2. Sec. 1639-2, G. C., 117 O. L., 520, effective Aug. 19, 1937. 

In re Januszewski, 196 Fed., 123. 3. 4. 

Peefer vs. State, 42 Ohio App., 276, 182 N. E., 117, 12 O. L. A., 583. 

Smith vs. State, 14 O. C. C. (N. S.), 257. 5. 

6. State vs. Wilcox, 26 O. N. P. (N. S.), 343. 7. 

Anss vs. State, 16 Ohio App., 502. 63

JUVENILE COURT 

selling or handling intoxicating liquors,8 immoral conduct,9 and habitual truancy are all act of delinquency. 

Some states except the most serious offenses from the category of juvenile delinquency. In others the same practical situation, or a worse one, results from giving to the juvenile court only concurrent jurisdiction, the criminal courts being allowed to deal in the old way with children brought before them. Such exceptions violate the fundamental principle that with children it is the personality and the whole situation that should be considered and dealt with, specific offenses being only symptoms which may or may not indicate the proper treatment. 

The juvenile court, with its social viewpoint, its facilities for investigation and specialized experience in handling children's problems, is the authority which should decide in every instance what disposition is necessary, and if any children are to be tried for crimes the discretion to permit it should rest with the judge of the juvenile court. 

SEC. 1639-3 G. C. NEGLECTED CHILD DEFINED. 

For the purpose of this chapter the words "neglected child" includes any child: 

1. Who is abandoned by its parents, guardian or custodian. 

2. Who lacks proper parental care by reason of the faults or habits of its parents, guardian or custodian. 

3. Whose parents, guardian or custodian neglect or refuse to provide it with proper or necessary subsistence, education, medical or surgical care, or other care necessary for its health, morals or well being. 

4. Whose parents, guardian or custodian neglect or refuse to provide the special care made necessary by its mental condition. 

5. Who is found in a disreputable place, or visits or patronizes a place prohibited by law; or who associates with vagrant, vicious, criminal, notorious or immoral persons. 

6. Who engages in an occupation prohibited by law, or is in a situation dangerous to life or limb or injurious to the health or morals of itself or others.10

DISCUSSION: 

This section includes the recommendation made by the 

Standard Code Committee. 

The term "neglected child" was undefined previous to the enactment of this section. A distinction is made, though some laws fail to do so, betweɛn "neglected" and "dependent" children, though the same child may be both neglected and delinquent as well. Neglect connotes a wilfully neglectful adult; while dependency connotes simply the inability of those legally responsible for a child to care for him, or the nonexistence of such persons. 

8. State vs. Butler, 25 O. O. 567, 11 Ohio Sup. 18. 

9. State vs. Hannawalt, 26 O. L. A. 641. 

10. Sec. 1639-3, G. C., 117 O. L., 520, effective Aug. 19, 1937. 64

It is now the intention to designate a child "neglected" in all cases where it was formerly delinquent or dependent due to the neglect or omission of a parent or custodian who is able to perform the duties imposed upon such parent or custodian by law. 

Failing to provide a child with proper education and refusal to comply with a vaiid rule relative to vaccination1 under former statutes made a child dependent. Now such neglect and refusal if the act of a parent makes a child neglected.

A child is not neglected or dependent simply because its father served a term for a felony where he did not fail to support the child and treated it humanely,2 nor is a child neglected merely because its parents are divorced.3 

SEC. 1639-4 G. C. DEPENDENT CHILD DEFINED. 

For the purpose of this chapter the words "dependent child" includes any child: 

1. Who is homeless or destitute or without proper care or support, through no fault of its parents, guardian or custodian. 2. Who lacks proper care or support by reason of the mental or physical condition of its parents, guardian or custodian. 

3. Whose condition or environment is such as to warrant the state, in the interests of the child, in assuming its guardianship.4

DISCUSSION: 

The first two paragraphs of this section are almost identical with the standard code.

Under this section, a child is "dependent" only in such cases in which it is in need of public care or guardianship through no fault of its parents, custodian or guardian. A child who is not furnished with proper care by its parents, who are able but neglect to do so, is "neglected" as defined in the next preceding section. 

An order of the juvenile court adjudging an iilegitimate child dependent at instance of the mother who desires to place such child for adoption to avoid or prevent disgrace upon herself is not a cause for a finding of dependency, and such finding may be set aside at her instance.5 

SEC. 1639-5. LACK OF PARENTAL CARE DEFINED. 

A child within the provisions of this chapter, whose home is filthy or unsanitary; or whose parents, step-parents, guardian or custodian permit it to become dependent, neglected or delinquent; or when able, refuse or neglect to provide it with 1. 

In re Hargy, 23 O. N. P. (N. S.), 129, 32 O. D. (N. P.), 8. 2. 

In re Konneker, 80 Ohio App., 502, 165 N. E., 850, 7 O. L. A., 137. 

3. Sonnenberg vs. State, 40 Ohio App., 475, 178 N. E., 855, 10 O. L. A., 271. 

4. Sec. 1639-4, G. C., 117 O. L., 520, effective Aug. 19, 1937. 

5. In re Hobson, 44 O. L. A. 86, 62 N. E. (2d) 510. 65

JUVENILE COURT 

necessary care, support, medical attention and educational facilities; or who fail to subject such child to necessary discipline; shali be deemed to be without proper parental cars or guardianship.6

DISCUSSION: 

The definition of "parental care" is now general in form instead of enumerating a number of specific instances which constituted lack of proper parental care. 

The fact that some might think that a child can be reared better by someone else is not sufficient to justify a court to divest parents of the custody of a child for not having proper parental care.7

SEC. 1639-6 G. C. RESIDENCE OR LEGAL SETTLEMENT DEFINED. 

For the purposes of this chapter, a child shall have the same residence or legal settlement as its parents, legal guardian of its person, or its custodian who stands in the relation of loco-parentis, except as otherwise provided for by statute.8 

DISCUSSION: 

Where a married woman leaves her husband in another state for his aggression she may establish a legal settlement in Ohio and the juvenile court may provide for her children if she is later committed to a state hospital.9 

The residence of a mental incompetent child should be determined by the state welfare department.10 

Formerly the legal residence of the child or its parents or those standing in loco parentis, did not determine the jurisdiction of the court.11 See Sec. 1639-34 G. C. for jurisdiction in cases where legal residence is involved. "In loco parentis" means in place of or instead of a parent.12 An illegitimate child has the same legal settlement as its mother.13

SEC. 1639-7a. COMPENSATION OF JUVENILE JUDGE; 

LIMITATION. 

In all counties where the state is not paying a salary direct to the judge exercising the powers and jurisdiction conferred in this chapter the state shall pay into the county treasury of the county, wherein such judge was elected, the sum of fifteen hundred dollars annually. The juvenile judge in such counties shall receive as his annual compensation fifteen hundred dollars. Provided that the combined salaries, allowances and compensation, of the probate judge and juvenile judge of said county shall not exceed the total salary provided by law for a common pleas judge in said county. Any unused portion of said fund shall remain in the county treasury to be used in the maintenance and operation of the juvenile court.3 

DISCUSSION: 

This section will not effect the compensation of any judge until there is a succeeding election or appointment. 

Under the limitation that the total salary shall not exceed the total provided by law for a common pleas judge, the salary of the probate and juvenile court judge who is now receiving compensation in excess of that paid the common pleas judge may be actually decreased until the common pleas judge receives the additional compensa tion provided by Section 2252 G. SEC. 1639-10 G. C. CONTEMPT. 

The court exercising the powers and jurisdiction conferred in this chapter shall have the same jurisdiction in contempt as courts of common pleas.7

DISCUSSION: 

Contempt of court is covered by sections 12136 to 12147, inclusive, of the General Codɛ. A person found guilty of contempt may be fined not to exceed $500.00 or imprisoned not to exceed 10 days or both, except if the contempt consists in the omission to do an act which the accused can yet perform he may be imprisoned until he performs it.

Although it has been held that a juvenile court has inherent power to punish violation of its orders as contempt of court,8 this section was enacted as recommended by the standard code committee, so the position of the court will be strengthened by express statutory authority.

SEC. 1639-11 G. C. RULES. 

The court shall have the power to prescribe rules not inconsistent with the laws of this state, regulating the docketing and hearing of causes, motions and demurrers, and such other matters as are necessary for the orderly conduct of its business and the prevention of delay, and for the government of its officers and employees, including their conduct, duties, hours, expenses, leaves of absence and vacations.9 

9. Sec. 1639-11, G. C., 121 O. L., 558, effective Jan. 1, 1946. 71

JUVENILE COURT SEC. 1639-12 G. C. TERMS OF COURT. 

Courts of common pleas, division of domestic relations, exercising powers and jurisdiction conferred in this chapter, shall have the same terms as courts of common pleas. Separate and independent juvenile courts shall have the terms provided by law for said courts. Juvenile courts within the probate court shall have the same terms as the probate court. 

All actions and other business under this chapter pending at the expiration of any term of court shall be deemed automatically continued without further notice. The judge may adjourn court or continue any case from day to day in the next term whenever, in his opinion, such continuance is warranted.

Sessions of the court may be held at such places throughout the county as the judge shall from time to time determine.10 

DISCUSSION: 

Courts of common pleas, under section 1533 G. C., hold at least three terms of court each year. The order fixing terms is required to be made on the third Tuesday of 

October for the next calendar year.

Under section 1683-24 G. C., the juvenile court of 

Cuyahoga County has four terms of three months each, commencing on the first day of January, April, July and 

October of each year. 

Probate courts, under section 10501-18 G. C., have three terms of four months each, commencing on the first law day of January, May and September of each year.

There is no specific provision authorizing a juvenile court to vacate or modify its judgments after term except that authorized by section 1639-35 G. C. and Sec. 

1639-49 G. C. 

Sec. 11575 et seq., G. C., relative to new trial, would probably apply to the prosecution of adults because an appeal on a question of fact (error) can be prosecuted to the court of appeals.

SEC. 1639-13 G. C. RECORDS; FORMS; ANNUAL REPORT. 

The court shall maintain records of all official cases brought before it, including an appearance docket, a journal and a cash book. The parent or parents of any child affected, if living, or if deceased, the nearest of kin, shall have the right during the hours in which said court is open to inspect such records, either in person or by counsel.

Not later than June of each year, the court shall prepare or cause the clerk to prepare an annual report covering the preceding calendar year, which report shall show the number and kinds of cases that have come before it, the disposition thereof ordered by the court, and such other data pertaining to the work of the court as the judge may direct or as the 10. 

Sec. 1639-12, G. C., 117 O. L., 520, effective Aug. 19, 1937. 72

state department of welfare may request. Copies of such report shall be filed with the county commissioners and with the state department of public welfare. 11 

DISCUSSION: 

As the juvenile court is made a court of record under Sec. 1639-7 G. C., a final record should be kept. 

SEC. 1639-14 G. C. SPECIAL ROOM FOR JUVENILE CASES. 

The county commissioners shall provide a special room not used for the trial of criminal or adult cases, when available, for the hearing of the cases of dependent, neglected and delinquent children.1

DISCUSSION: 

This section follows the recommendation of the standard code committee. In order to hold court apart from a criminal atmosphere, many judges of juvenile courts hold sessions in very inadequate quarters at considerable inconvenience to themselves, rather than have children. brought into an adult court room.

Suitable quarters should be conveniently located, sufficiently commodious and comfortable and arranged to afford not only convenience but real privacy to children and family groups.

SEC. 1639-15 G. C. 

SEPARATE BUILDING AND SITE MAY BE PURCHASED, OR LEASED. 

Upon the advice and recommendation of the judge of the court exercising the powers and jurisdiction conferred in this chapter, the county commissioners may provide by purchase, lease or otherwise, a separate building and site to be known as "the juvenile court" at a convenient location within the county which shall be appropriately constructed, arranged, furnished and maintained for the convenient and efficient transaction of the business of the court and all parts thereof and its employees including adequate facilities to be used as laboratories, dispensaries, or clinics for the use of scientific specialists connected with the court.2

DISCUSSION: 

A former similar section was used to build a separate juvenile court building in Cuyahoga County. 

SEC. 1639-16 G. C. JURISDICTION OF COURT. 

(a) The court shall have exclusive original jurisdiction under this chapter or under other provisions of the General 

Code: 

1. Concerning any child who is (1) delinquent, (2) neglected, (3) dependent, crippled, or otherwise physically handicapped. 

11. Sec. 1639-13 G. C., 121 O. L., 558, effective Jan. 1, 1946. 

1. Sec. 1639-14, G. C., 117 O. L., 520, effective Aug. 19, 1937. 

2. Sec. 1639-15, G. C., 117 O. L., 529, effective Aug. 19, 1937. 73

JUVENILE COURT 

2. To determine the custody of any child not a ward of another court. 

3. To determine the paternity of any child alleged to have been born out of wedlock and to provide for the support of such child, subject to the concurrent jurisdiction of other courts as provided by law.

Adults Charged 

(b) The court shall have original jurisdiction to determine all cases of misdemeanors charging adults (1) with contributing to, encouraging, or tending to cause by any act or omission, the delinquency, neglect or dependency of any child; or (2) with any act or omission with respect to any child, which act or omission is a violation of any state law or any municipal ordinance; or (3) with desertion, abandonment or failure to provide subsistence to any child for which he is legally responsible.

May Try Divorce Cases 

(c) Except as to juvenile courts separately and independently created by law, jurisdiction to hear, determine and make a record of any action for divorce or alimony involving the custody or care of children filed in the court of common pleas and certified by the presiding judge of the common pleas court with all the papers filed therein to the court for trial. 

Provided, however, that no such certification shall be made to any court unless the consent of the judge of the court exercising the powers and jurisdiction conferred in this chapter is first obtained. After such certification is made and consent obtained, the court shall proceed as if such action was originally begun in said court, except as to awards for alimony or support due and unpaid at the time of certification, over which the juvenile court shall have no jurisdiction. (d) The court shall have jurisdiction to hear and determine the case of any child duly certified to the court according to law by any court of competent jurisdiction, and to make disposition of said child in accordance with the provisions of this chapter.3 

See Sec. 8034-1 G. C. 3. 4. 

DISCUSSION: 

(a) Exclusive Jurisdiction. The word "exclusive" as used in this section only confers exclusive jurisdiction from the standpoint of the child.4 It vests exclusive jurisdiction over the infant, not the crime.5

The Juvenile Court has exclusive original jurisdiction over delinquent, neglected, dependent, crippled or otherwise physically handicapped children, and if a complaint is filed in the Juvenile Court alleging that a child is delinquent, neglected, dependent, crippled, or otherwise 

Sec. 1639-16 G. C., 121 O. L., 558, effective Jan. 1, 1946. 

In re Cooper, 134 O. S. 40, 15 N. E. (2d) 634; In re Howell, 21 O. O. 379, 7 Ohio Sup. 4. 

5. In re Evans, 67 Ohio App. 66, 35 N. E. (2d) 887. 74

physically handicapped, the Juvenile Court may exercise such jurisdiction if the complaint is proven and may make such order as provided by the Juvenile Court code even though the child is a ward of the Common Pleas Court in a divorce proceeding, except that it may not change the custody of said child as between the parties. to the divorce action unless the question of custody is certified to the Juvenile Court by the court granting the divorce decree.6 (b) Delinquent Children. From the fact that this section gives the court exclusive original jurisdiction over delinquent children and with the definition of a delinquent child as defined in section 1639-2 G. C., it would seem that other courts would without further stipulation be deprived of criminal jurisdiction over children, but the effect of similar provisions has been questioned in Minnesota7 and in Colorado.8

To remove any question as to whether the juvenile court has exclusive jurisdiction, section 1639-29 has been enacted, which provides that when a criminal charge is filed against a child in another court, it must be transferred to the juvenile court.9 

(c) Dependent Children. The inclusion of dependent children in the court's jurisdiction is in accordance with the law in most states. The reason, however, for such inclusion has usually been the lack of other machinery well adapted to the administration of public relief to children. 

In a few eastern states the juvenile courts have no jurisdiction over dependent children as such. This is done on the theory that the administration of relief is not a judicial function. 

A parent's right to custody of a child over two years. of age may be relinquished by contract, forfeited by abandonment, or lost by being in a condition of total inaiblity to afford it necessary care and support.10 

Formerly a juvenile court did not have jurisdiction to render a money judgment11 for past support. It now has such power under Sec. 1639-34 G. C. (d) Concurrent Jurisdiction. Where two courts are by law vested with concurrent jurisdiction as to the same subject matter, the court which first acquires the jurisdiction of persons involved in litigation within such concurrent jurisdiction may proceed with the trial or disposition of such cause and make such orders therein as may be required without interference from any other court with like powers.12 

6. In re Jones, 33 O. O. 331, 46 O. L. A. 132, 668 N. E. (2d) 97. 

7. State vs. Peterson, 153 Minn., 310.

Pesta vs Pesta, 45 O. L. A. 631, 68 N. E. (2d) 234. 10. 11. 12. 75

JUVENILE COURT 

A divorce court cannot assume jurisdiction over children which have been previously made wards of the juvenile court. 13 Likewise, a juvenile court cannot determine the custody of a child the ward of another court unless the custody is certified to the juvenile court as provided in this section; but if a child is deiinquent, neglected, dependent, crippled or otherwise physically handicapped the juvenile court may exercise its jurisdiction except it may not change the custody of said child as between its parents unless the court having prior jurisdiction certifies the question of custody and support to the juvenile court.14

Before a court of common pleas can certify a case of children to the juvenile court, the consent of the juvenile judge must be first obtained.1 When the court of common pleas surrenders jurisdiction over a child to the juvenile court, it loses its jurisdiction and the juvenile court has continuing and exclusive jurisdiction thereafter.2 (e) Children Born Out of Wedlock. The juvenile court under this section is given concurrent jurisdiction over children born out of wedlock and to determine paternity. 

These children especially need the protection of a socialized court in order that their cases may not be disposed of on the basis of a money compromise with little regard for the safeguarding of the child's future. See section 

12110 G. C., et seq., for a full discussion. (f) Adults. Jurisdiction over adults for the offenses named is best exercised in many cases by the court dealing with children. The court which first uncovers the parental neglect or non-support or the exploitation of children or other offenses against them should have authority to deal also with the vicious influences which, unchecked, would nullify all efforts to protect children in their own homes.

The evolution of the juvenile court to a family court is a natural one. Juvenile courts necessarily deal with adults involved in children's cases. This section gives the court authority to deal with such adults directly and effectively. Cases against adults often differ from children's cases in legal title only. For their right adjustment they call for similar methods and the same social viewpoint. 

(g) Supplemental Jurisdiction Delinquent Children. 

The state department of public welfare may, when willing to do so, receive as its wards with all the powers

Guardianship By State (h) Supplemental Jurisdiction Neglected and Depend end Children. The state department of public welfare must, when able to do so, receive as its wards such dependent or neglected minors as may be committed to it by the juvenile court. County, district, or semi-public children's homes or any institution entitled to receive children from the juvenile court or the board of administration may, with the consent of the board, transfer to it the guardianship of minor wards of such institutions or board. If such children have been committed to such institutions or to the board of administration by the juvenile court, that court must first consent to such transfer. 

The board shall thereupon ipso facto become vested with the sole and exclusive guardianship of such child or children.4

State Aid Provided (i) Supplemental Jurisdiction Crippled Children. In order to provide suitable medical and surgical treatment, and education when necessary, of crippled children whose parents or guardians fail or are financially unable to provide such treatment, the state department of public welfare is authorized and empowered to receive into its custody such children. Application for such care, treatment, and education, must first be made to the juvenile court by a parent, guardian or some interested person. If such court is of the opinion that such child is in need of treatment and education, and finds that the parent or guardian fails to provide it, he may make an order to that end; or if the parent or guardian is financially unable to pay all or a part of the expense of such treatment, the court shall make a proper finding and decree. In either case the court must at once forward a copy of the decree and a statement of facts to the department of public welfare, and such department must, when able to do so, accept such child for care. Upon receipt of notice from such board that such child can be given suitable treatment the court must then commit such child to such department and provide for its conveyance in charge of a suitable person to the place designated by such department for treatment. The expenses for conveyance shall be paid by the county or by the parent or guardian as the court may direct. Such commitment is temporary and is only for the period necessary for the treatment of such

The state department of public welfare must arrange for the treatment and education of crippled children committed to it by the juvenile court. The expenses for board, clothing and personal necessities and for mental, medical, surgical, dental, and optical examination and treatment, including massaging and other beneficial treatment and braces, artificial limbs and accessories and their upkeep, and for education when necessary must be paid out of funds appropriated to the use of the department by the general assembly; but the board of state charities may require parents or guardians to pay the state for such expenses when in its judgment such action is just, and if the parents are unable to pay, the cost is charged back to the county and paid out of the general fund in the county treasury.8

Truants From School 

(j) Supplemental Jurisdiction Truants. If the parent, guardian or other person in charge of a child, upon complaint for a failure to cause the child to attend school or a part-time school or class, proves inability to do so, then he must be discharged, and thereupon the attendance officer must make complaint before the judge of the juvenile court of the county that the child is a delinquent child or dependent child within the meaning of the law. 

Such judge must hear the complaint and if he determines that the child is a delinquent or dependent child' within the meaning of the law relative to dependent and delinquent children, he must dispose of such child as provided by law.1 

(k) Supplemental Jurisdiction Adults. In addition to the jurisdiction conferred in adult cases under sections 

1639-37, 1639-45 and 1639-46 G. C., it is believed that under this section juvenile courts have jurisdiction of offenses that are misdemeanors covered by other sections.

SEC. 1639-18 G. C. APPOINTMENT OF PROBATION OF- FICERS AND OTHER EMPLOYEES. 

4. Sec. 1639-17, G. C., 117 O. L., 520, effective Aug. 19, 1937.

The judge may appoint such bailiffs, probation officers and other employees as may be necessary and may designate their titles and fix their duties, compensation and expense allowances. The court may by entry on its journal authorize any deputy clerk to administer oaths when necessary in the discharge of their duties. Such employees shall serve during the pleasure of the judge. 80

The compensation and expenses of all employees and the salary and expenses of the judge shall be paid in semi-monthly installments by the county treasurer from the money appropriated for the operation of the court, upon the warrant of the county auditor, certified to by the judge of the court. 

The judge may require any court employee to give bond in the sum of not less than $1,000.00, conditioned for the honest and faithful performance of his or her duties. The sureties on such bonds shall be approved in the manner provided for the approval and filing of the bond of the clerk. The judge shall not be personally liable for the default, misfeasance or non-feasance of any employee from whom a bond has been required as herein provided.5

DISCUSSION: 

This section is similar to former section 1662 G. C., which provided that probation officers should serve "during the pleasure of the judge" and with the recommendation of the standard code committee that "all employees may be removed by the judge."

The judge should have sufficient latitude as to selection and compensation in order to exercise real responsibility for the success of the court. Giving the judge power to appoint probation officers has been held constitutional.6 Giving the board of county commissioners power to make the appointment of probation officers has been held unconstitutional.7 

Competitive examinations in order to eliminate unqualified applicants would seem to be desirable. 

Because this section provides that such employees shall serve "during the pleasure of the judge,' such employees are exempt from civil service.8 If under civil service and appointed as one of the exemptions of the judge, such service terminates with the expiration of the term of the judge who made the appointment.9 The county commissioners must appropriate such sums as may be necessary to pay the salaries of the employees appointed.10 5. 6. 7. 8. 

The offices of a member of the county board of education,11 and chief of police 12 are incompatible with that of a probation officer, but a relief administrator is not.13

SEC. 1639-19 G. C. DUTIES AND POWERS OF PROBA- TION DEPARTMENT. 

The chief probation officer, under the direction of the judge, shall have charge of the work of the probation department. 

The probation department shall make such investigations as the court may direct, keep a written record of such investigations and submit the same to the judge or deal with them as he may direct. The department shall furnish to any person placed on probation a statement of the conditions of probation and shall instruct him regarding them. The department shall keep informed concerning the conduct and condition of each person under its provision and shall report thereon to the judge as he may direct. Each probation officer shall use all suitable methods to aid persons on probation and to bring about improvement in their conduct and condition. The department shall keep full records of its work; shall keep accurate and complete accounts of money collected from persons under its supervision, shall give receipts therefor and shall make reports thereon as the judge may direct. 

Reports Are Confidential

The reports and records of the department shall be considered as confidential information and shall not be made public. A probation officer shall have power to serve the warrants and other process of the court within or without the county, and may make arrests without warrant upon reasonable information or upon view of the violation of any of the provisions of this chapter, detain the person so arrested pending the issuance of a warrant, and perform such other duties, incident to their offices, as the judge directs. All sheriffs, deputy sheriffs, constables, marshals, chiefs of police and other police officers shall render assistance to probation officers, in the performance of their duties when requested so to do, by any probation officer.14

DISCUSSION: 

Somewhat detailed directions as to the duties of probation officers have been found useful in stimulating the organization of an adequate and efficient system, though they can do no more than to suggest in general terms the real business of probation.

In some states probation officers are also charged with the duty of supervising persons on parole from state institutions, on request of the latter, thus forming local. probation and parole departments. In Wisconsin the state probation and parole departments have been combined.

The probation law of the Supreme Bench of Baltimore, Maryland, has the following valuable provision, relating to adult probation work, but equally applicable to juvenile: "All information and data obtained in the discharge. 

14. Sec. 1639-19, G. C., 117 O. L., 520, effective Aug. 19, 1937.

ON 83

JUVENILE COURT 

of official duty by any worker in the probate department shall be privileged information and shall not be receivable as evidence and shall not be disclosed outside the membership of the probation department to anyone other than to a judge of this court unless and until otherwise ordered by the court or a judge thereof."

Standard forms for use by probation officers have been devised by a committee of the National Probation Association and are distributed by the Association at cost. 

If a writ is prima facie regular, a probation officer is exempt from liability for serving such writ even if insufficient in law,15
