SEC. 1639-20 G. C. WHEN POWERS AND DUTIES SHALL BE VESTED OR IMPOSED ON COUNTY DEPART- MENT OF PROBATION. 

When a county department of probation has been established in the county and the judge does not establish a probation department within the juvenile court as provided herein, all powers and duties of the probation department provided for in this chapter shail vest in and be imposed upon such county department of probation. 

In counties in which a county department of probation has been or is hereafter established the judge of the juvenile court may transfer to such department all or any part of the powers and duties of his own probation department; provided that all juvenile cases shall be handled within a county department of probation exclusively by an officer or division. separate and distinct from the officers or division handling adult cases.1 

15. Brinkman vs. Drolesbaugh, 97 O. S., 171, 119 N. E., 451. 

1. Sec. 1639-20, G. C., 121 O. L., 560, effective Jan. 1, 1946.

SEC. 1639-21 G. C. APPOINTMENT OF REFEREES. 

The judge may appoint referees, and fix their compensation, who shall have the usual power of masters in chancery cases, provided, however, in all such cases submitted to them by the court, they shall hear the testimony of witnesses and certify 84

to the judge of such court their findings upon the case submitted to them, together with their recommendation as to the judgment or order to be made in the case in question. The court, after notice to the parties in the case of the presentation of such findings and recommendations, may make the order recommended by the referee, or any other order in the judgment of the court required by the findings of the referee, or may hear additional testimony, or may set aside said findings and hear the case anew. In appointing a referee for the trial of females, a female referee shall be appointed where possible.2

DISCUSSION: 

No order can be properly made by the court upon the findings and recommendations of the referee without notice to the parties as required by this section. An oral statement of the referee to a party of what the findings and recommendations would be is not sufficient.3 

See Section 1639-18 G. C., for form appointing referee.

SEC. 1639-22 G. C. PLACE OF DETENTION. 

No child under eighteen years of age shall be placed in or committed to any prison, jail or lock-up, nor shall such child be brought into any police station, vehicle or other place where such child can come in contact or communication with any adult convicted of crime or under arrest and charged with crime; provided, that a child fourteen years of age or older, may, for good cause shown, and with the consent of the judge or a person designated by him, be placed in a jail or other place of detention for adults, but in a room or ward separate from adults.

Upon the advice and recommendation of the judge of the court exercising the powers and jurisdiction conferred in this chapter, the county commissioners shall provide, by purchase or lease or otherwise, a place to be known as a detention home within a convenient distance of the court, not used for the confinement of adult persons charged with criminal offenses, where delinquent, dependent or neglected children may be detained until final disposition, which home shall be maintained as provided in this act, or shall provide funds for the boarding of such children temporarily in private homes. Children who are alleged or adjudged delinquent, dependent or neglected may, after complaint is filed, be detained in such detention home or certified foster homes, until final disposition of their case. The court may arrange for the boarding of such children in certified foster homes, or in uncertified foster homes for a period not exceeding sixty days, subject to the 

2. Sec. 1639-21, G. C., 117 O. L., 520, effective Aug. 19, 1937. 3. 

In re Hobson, 44 O. L. A. 86, 62 N. E. (2d) 510. 85

JUVENILE COURT 

supervision of the court, or may arrange with any county department, child welfare board or certified organization to receive for temporary care children within the jurisdiction of the court.

In case a detention home is established as an agency of the court it shall be furnished and carried on, as far as possible, as a family home in charge of a superintendent or matron. 

The judge may appoint a superintendent, a matron and other necessary employees for such home in the same manner as is provided for the appointment of other employees of the court, their salaries to be fixed and paid in the same manner as the salaries of other employees. The necessary expenses incurred in maintaining such detention home shall be paid by the county.

In case the court shall arrange for the board of children temporarily detained in such foster homes, or through any private, certified organization a reasonable sum to be fixed by the court for the board of such children shall be paid by the county. In order to have such foster homes available for service an agreed monthly subsidy may be paid and a fixed rate per day for care of children actually residing therein.4 

DISCUSSION: 

With the present lack of adequate facilities for detention and for segregating different groups of children in most jurisdictions, and with the court's jurisdiction extending to the age of eighteen, it is impracticable to make iilegal all jail detention. If such detention is permitted only on order of the court and only for children over fourteen, the result will be a great improvement over existing conditions in most states. 

Sec. 3169 G. C., also prohibits minors under sixteen being placed in the same cell in a county jail with adults.

Detention facilities are as much a part of the court's resources for dealing with children as is the probation service; its administration should be as directly controiled by the court. Only in this way can a smooth working machine be assured, without conflicts of authority.

The use of private homes for detention homes has been found highly satisfactory.

Boards of education can not exclude children in a detention home from the public schools,5 and they must provide educational facilities and teachers for the detention home at the expense of the county.6 The superintendent of the detention home cannot be paid a fixed amount per meal for the maintenance of children in the home, but may be allowed the actual amount expended therefor.7 

4. Sec. 1639-22 G. C., 121 O. L. 560, effective Jan. 1, 1946. 

5. 1946 Opin. Atty. Gen., No. 868. 6. 

1946 Opin. Atty. Gen., No. 1200. 

7. 1930 Opin. Atty. Gen., No. 1489. 86

If recommended by the judge, the county commissioners must establish a detention home by purchase or lease. 

Such a detention home may be maintained at the county home for the aged if convenient to the juvenile court.8 

Where a zoning ordinance establishes an area called a dwelling-house district in which are authorized singlefamily dwellings and defines a family as "any number of individuals living together as a single house-keeping unit and doing their cooking on the premises" and further provides as an accessory use in such dwelling-house district that "in a dwelling or apartment occupied as a private residence one or more rooms may be rented or tableboard furnished," the temporary placing by the Juvenile Court of four or fewer children for care in a private home in a single residence in said district as provided by this section is not a violation of the provisions aforesaid of such zoning ordinance.9

For journal entry appointing superintendent or matron, and voucher for expenses, see forms for employees under Sec. 1639-18 G. C.

PROCEDURE IN CASES OF CHILDREN SEC. 1639-23 G. C. HOW COMPLAINT INVOLVING CHILD FILED. 

Any person having knowledge of a child under eighteen years of age who appears to be delinquent, neglected, or dependent, or of a crippled or otherwise physically handicapped child under 21 years of age, may, with respect to such delinquent, neglected or dependent child, file a sworn complaint, and with respect to such crippled or otherwise physically handicapped child file an app.ication for care in the court exercising the powers and jurisdiction conferred in this chapter, of the county in which such child has a residence or legal settlement, or in which such delinquency, neglect or dependency occurred, or, in case of a crippied child, where such child is found. Such application or sworn complaint may be upon information and belief, and shall be sufficiently definite by using the word delinquent, neglected, dependent, crippled, or physically handicapped, as the facts may be.10

DISCUSSION: 

In the absence of statutory regulations, conflicts of jurisdiction between different juvenile courts can be settled only by the application of the principle of priority.11 

This section specifies definitely in what county a proceeding can be brought.

The use of the word "petition" as recommended by the standard committee in instituting a proceeding was not 8. 

1938 Opin. Atty. Gen., No. 2804. 

9. 1944 Opin. Atty. Gen., No. 7011. 

10. Sec. 1639-23 G. C., 121 O. L. 561, effective Jan. 1, 1946. 

11. In re Bower, 78 Ore., 390. 87

JUVENILE COURT 

adopted in this section because that term is used to institute a civil law suit in this state. As criminal proceedings are instituted in Ohio by an affidavit, information or indictment, it was thought advisable to continue the use of the word "complaint" in instituting a case involving a child, if it was deemed a proceeding other than a law suit. 

As the procedure is not criminal the complaint need not have the particularity of an indictment.12 

Under the former law, it was held that when a divorce court had jurisdiction over a child, a complaint alleging dependency or delinquency could not be filed in the juvenile court.13 While a juvenile court cannot determine the custody of such child between parents unless the divorce court releases jurisdiction, the juvenile court may have jurisdiction over such child if it is dependent, neglected or delinquent because of the exclusive original jurisdiction of the juvenile court under Sec. 1639-16 G. C. 

Whenever a child is alleged to be delinquent, neglected or dependent, evidence may be received on ali matters pertaining to that question, even though all facts of delinquency, neglect or dependency are not alleged in the complaint.14

SEC. 1639-24 G. C. CITATION; SUBPOENAS. 

After a complaint or an application for care shall have been filed and after such further investigation as the court may direct, unless the parties voluntarily appear, the court shall issue a citation reciting briefly the substance of the complaint or application and requiring the parents, guardian, or the person or persons who have the custody or control of the child, or with whom it may be, to appear personally and bring the child before the court at the time and place stated. If the rson so cited shall be other than the parent or guardian of the child, then the parent or guardian, or both, shall also be notified of the pendency of the case and of the time and place of hearing.

Before any temporary commitment shall be made permanent the court shall fix a time and place for hearing and shall cause notice thereof to be served upon the parent or guardian of the child, or published, as provided in the next succeeding section.

Any person whose presence is considered necessary and not cited, may be subpoenaed to appear and testify at the hearing. Any one cited or subpoenaed to appear who fails to do so, may be punished as in other cases in the common pleas court, for contempt of court. Persons subpoenaed shall be paid the same witness fees as are allowed in the court of common pleas. 

If it appears that the child is in such conditions or surroundings that his welfare requires that his custody be immediately assumed by the court, the judge may cause to be endorsed upon the citation an order that the officer serving the same shall take the child into custody at once.15

DISCUSSION: 

However free from technicalities the hearings may be, scrupulous regard must be had for the rights of parents or other legal custodians of children to be duly notified and given an opportunity to be heard.1 The policy of the juvenile court is to carry this principle a step further and make parents or custodians entirely responsible, whenever practicable, for bringing the child to court as provided in this section. 

The words "person or persons who have the custody or control of the child, or with whom it may be," means a person having custody by operation of law or awarded to such person by judicial order.2 

15. Sec. 1639-24 G. C., 121 O. L. 561, effective Jan. 1, 1946. 

In re Becknell, 119 Cal., 496. 1. 

2. Rarey vs. Schmidt, 115 O. S., 518, 154 N. E., 914, 5 O. L. A., 12. 89

JUVENILE COURT 

A voluntary appearance at a hearing constitutes a waiver of citation, notice or subpoena.3 

Lack of proper notice makes a finding subject to collateral attack.4 Notice is now required even though a warrant is issued for a child.

Unless notice is given to the parents, the jurisdiction of the court does not attach and a judgment of commitment rendered in such proceeding is void. 

Parents are the legal and natural custodians of their minor children, and each parent has an equal right to such custody in the absence of an order, judgment or decree of a court of competent jurisdiction fixing their custody.5 

(Title) 

Jurisdiction is not acquired over a child by citing its parents on a charge of neglect and failure to provide.6 

Formerly an original order made after notice and hearing could be modified without further notice.7 Now a temporary commitment cannot be made permanent without notice and further hearing.

SEC. 1639-25 G. C. SERVICE OF CITATIONS, NOTICES AND SUBPOENAS. 

Service of citations, notices and subpoenas shall be made by delivering a copy to the person cited, notified or subpoenaed; or by leaving a copy at his usual place of residence; provided, that if the judge is satisfied that such service is impracticable, he may order service by registered mail. 

Whenever it shall appear from affidavit that a parent or guardian or other person having the custody of such child resides or has gone out of the state or that his or her place of residence is unknown so that such citation cannot be served on him or her, the clerk shall cause such citation to be published once in a newspaper of general circulation throughout the county, and published in the county, if there be one so published. The citation shall state the substance and the time and place of the hearing, which shall be held at least one week later than the date of the publication; and a copy of such citation shall be sent by mail to the last known address of such parent, guardian or other person having custody of such child, unless said affidavit shows that a reasonable effort has been made without success to ascertain such address. 

The certificate of the clerk that such publication has been made or such citation mailed shall be sufficient evidence thereof. When such period of one week from the time of publication shall have elapsed, said court shall have full jurisdiction to deal with such child as provided by this chapter.8 92

DISCUSSION: 

If personal service cannot be had, notice by publication is jurisdictional.9 Habeas corpus will lie to test the sufficiency of constructive service.10

SEC. 1639-27 G. C. RELEASE OF CHILD PENDING HEARING. 

Whenever any officer takes a child into custody, he shall, unless it is impracticable or has been otherwise ordered by the court, accept the written promise of the parent, guardian or custodian, to bring the child to the court at the time fixed. 

Thereupon such child may be released in the custody of a parent, guardian or custodian. If not so released, such child shall be placed in the custody of a probation officer or other person designated by the court, or taken immediately to the court or to the place of detention designated by the court, and the officer taking it shall immediately notify the court with a written report of the condition or situation which caused the child to be brought in and shall file a complaint when directed to do so by the court. 

In the case of any child whose custody has been assumed by the court and pending the final disposition of the case, the child may be released in the custody of a parent, guardian or custodian, or of a probation officer or other person appointed 

Sec. 1639-26, G. C., 117 O. L., 520, effective Aug. 19, 1937. 94

by the court, to be brought before the court at the time designated. When not released as herein provided, such child, pending the hearing of the case, shall be detained in such place of detention as shall be designated by the court, subject to further order.

Nothing herein contained shall be construed as forbidding any peace officer, police officer or probation officer or other person authorized by the judge of the juvenile court from taking into custody any child who is found violating any law or ordinance or who is reasonably believed to be a fugitive from his parents or from justice, or whose surroundings are such as to endanger his health, morals or welfare. No child shall be fingerprinted or caused or permitted to be fingerprinted or photographed by any officer or other person holding him in custody without the consent of the judge of the juvenile court. When the judge of the juvenile court permits the fingerprinting of any child, the prints shall be taken on a civilian and not on a criminal card and shall be kept only in the civilian file. Provided, however, that any child recognized to the court of common pleas, under the provisions of section 

1639-32, shall not be exempt from fingerprinting and photographing. In every such case the officer taking such child into custody shall immediately report the fact to the court and the case shall then be proceeded with as provided in this chapter.12

DISCUSSION: 

This section conforms to that recommended by the standard code committee. It was enacted so as to prevent the unnecessary arrest or detention of children, especially detention in jails or police stations, even for a few hours. Police officers and others need education on this point. The important thing is to impress on officers' minds that there must be the least possible delay in getting word to the juvenile court when a child has been taken into custody. The court should assume actual jurisdiction as soon as possible.

The strict enforcement of this section will serve to emphasize the policy of the juvenile court to enforce parental responsibility for children. Most states have similar though often inadequate provisions, without mention of any security. These constitute one of the most important differences between criminal and chancery procedure. Under the former the child would be held in jail unless he could give bond for his appearance.

This section does not violate the provisions of the constitution relating to bail.1

Confessions made by a child under eighteen years of age to juvenile court authorities may be used against him in the court of common pleas on a charge of murder

SEC. 1639-28 G. C. PROVISIONS FOR TEMPORARY CARE AND TREATMENT. 

Pending hearing and service of citations, the court may make such temporary disposition of any child as it may deem best. Upon the certificate of one or more reputable practicing physicians, the court may summarily provide for emergency medical and surgical treatment as appears to be immediately necessary, for any child concerning or in behalf of whom a complaint or an application for care has been filed, pending the service of a citation upon its parents, guardian or custodian. The court may order the parents, guardian or custodian, if found able to do so, to reimburse the court for the expense involved in providing such emergency medical and surgical treatment, and any such person who disobeys such 

2. State vs. Lowder, 79 Ohio App. 237, 79 N. E. (2d) 785. 96

order may be adjudged in contempt of court and may be punished accordingly. 

When such emergency medical and surgical treatment is furnished a child who is found at the hearing to be a nonresident of a county, and if the expense of such medical and surgical treatment cannot be recovered from the parents, legal guardian or custodian of such child, the county commissioners of the county in which such child has a legal settlement shall reimburse the court for the reasonable cost of such emergency medical or surgical treatment, out of its county general fund.3

DISCUSSION: 

This section was enacted in order to give emergency medical and surgical treatment to children pending hearing.

Juvenile Judge.

SEC. 1639-29 G. C. TRANSFER OF CASE FILED IN AN- OTHER COURT. 

When a child is arrested on and under any charge, complaint, affidavit, or indictment, whether for a felony or a misdemeanor, such child shall be taken directly before the juvenile court. If the child is taken before a justice of the peace, mayor, judge of the police or municipal court or court of common pleas other than a juvenile court, it shall be the duty of such justice of the peace, mayor or such judge of the police or municipal court or court of common pleas to transfer the case to the court exercising the powers and jurisdiction herein provided. The officers having such child in charge shall take it before the judge of such court, who shall proceed to hear and dispose of the case in the same manner as if the child had been brought before such judge in the first instance. Upon such transfer or taking of such child before such judge, all further proceedings upon or under the charge, complaint, information or indictment shall be discontinued in the court of said justice of the peace, mayor, police or municipal judge or judge of the court of common pleas other than a court exercising the powers and jurisdiction herein conferred, and the case against or relating to such child shall thenceforth be within the exclusive jurisdiction of such court and shall be deemed to be upon a complaint filed in such court 

3. Sec. 1639-28 G. C., 121 O. L. 563, effective Jan. 1, 1946. 97

JUVENILE COURT 

as fully as if the appearance of such child had been upon a complaint filed in and a citation or warrant of arrest originally issued out of and by such court.4 

DISCUSSION: 

Children in some jurisdictions are taken before other courts by mistake. It is important to have this section as recommended by the standard code committee which provides for immediate transfer to the juvenile court. 

In many states (e.g., Colorado, Illinois, Louisiana, Missouri, New York, West Virginia) violations of city ordinances are not crimes. California, to the contrary, has no intermediate classification between criminal and civil cases, and Michigan, as Ohio, hold a violation of an ordinance to be a "crime or misdemeanor."5

As the juvenile court has exclusive jurisdiction over minors under eighteen years of age,6 a child under eighteen who has been arrested or convcited in another court should challenge the jurisdiction of the court before the motion for new trial, or he may be barred. His age may be interposed in a plea of abatement,7 and if refused, error should be prosecuted.8 The holding that a child who commits a criminal offense a few days before he becomes eighteen need not be brought before the juvenile court if taken into custody by another court after he becomes eighteen, may now be erroreous because of the provisions of section 1639-30 G. C.9 A child under eighteen who is extradited from another state must first be taken to the juvenile court.10

SEC. 1639-30 G. C. HEARING; JUDGMENT. 

The court may conduct the hearing in an informal manner, and may adjourn the hearing from time to time. In the hearing of any case the general public may be excluded and on.y such persons admitted as have a direct interest in the case. 

All cases involving children shall be heard separately and apart from the trial of cases against adults. The court, at its discretion, may excuse the attendance of the child at the hearing in cases involving neglected, dependent or crippled children. The court shall hear and determine all cases of children without a jury. If the court shall find that the child is delinquent, neglected, or dependent, it may by order duly entered proceed as follows: 

1. Place the child on probation or under supervision in its

2. Whenever a child commits an act or acts of delinquency before arriving at the age of eighteen years, and the specific complaint thereon is not filed or hearing held until after said child arrives at the age of eighteen years, the court shall have jurisdiction to hear and dispose of such complaint, the same as if the complaint were filed and hearing held before such child arrived at the age of eighteen years, provided that no child over eighteen years of age at the time of the court hearing may be committed to the boys' or girls' industrial schools. 

The judgment rendered by the court under this section shall not impose any of the civil disabilities ordinarily imposed by conviction, in that the child shall not be deemed a criminal by reason of such adjudication, nor shall any child be charged or convicted of a crime in any court, except as provided in section 1639-32, General Code. The disposition of a child under the judgment rendered or any evidence given in the court shall not be admissible as evidence against the child in any other case or proceeding in any other court except that the judgment rendered and the disposition of such child may be considered by any court only as applies to the matter of sentence or to the granting of probation. Such disposition or evidence shall not operate to disqualify a child in any future civil service examination, appointment or application.

Whenever the court shall commit a child to the division, the state department of public welfare or to any department, board, institution or agency it shall transmit with the order of commitment an adequate case history of the child and its family.

With respect to a crippled or otherwise physically handicapped child, who is neither delinquent, neglected or depend- 99

JUVENILE COURT 

ent, the court may commit the child temporarily to the division or to any agency in Ohio, authorized and qualified to provide or secure the care, treatment or placement required in the particular case.11 

(a) Formality of Hearing. 

If it is demanded, the hearing for a child must be conducted upon evidence introduced in the usual and ordinary course of the administration of justice,1 but such procedure does not inure to benefit of the child. If the hearing can be kept as informal as possible, and if the judge can appear in the role of friend, instead of dealing with the child as a culprit, better and lasting results are always obtained. (b) 

Evidence. 

Confessions obtained from a juvenile before a complaint has been filed may be used in the hearing.2

Admitting into evidence an order of the juvenile court in a criminal proceeding against the same person is prejudical error,3 but when a defendant in a criminal case is permitted to introduce evidence of his life history, he waives the protection of this section and may be cross examined as to charges preferred against him as a juvenile.4 

A confession obtained by juvenile court authorities may be introduced in evidence in the court of common pleas where the case was referred to the common pleas court for trial.5 

Commitment of Child. (c) The judge should make oniy such disposition of the child as will do it the most good. Commitment to an institution should be made only as a last resort. The rights of the parents and others is secondary to the welfare of the child. 

DISCUSSION: 

The court may divide the custody of a child under its jurisdiction, between its parents on a temporary order without losing jurisdiction, and if neglected or dependent may thereafter commit such child to a children's home or make other disposition thereof.6

The court has power to place a child with relative or other fit person outside of the State and charge the expense of transportation to the county.7 Children placed in foster homes, with or without remuneration, are entitled to free school privileges where they reside.8 1. 2. 3. 4. 5. 6. 7.

(f) Hearing After Child Becomes Eighteen Years of Age. 

Before it was specifically provided in this section that the court should have jurisdiction over a child after it became eighteen for an act committed before arriving at eighteen as recommended by the standard code committee, a difficult question arose as to jurisdiction in such cases. One state held that the juvenile court should take jurisdiction,3 while another held that in such cases the juvenile court had no jurisdiction.4

When a child commits an act or acts of delinquency before arriving at the age of eighteen years, and the complaint is not filed or hearing held until after said child arrives at the age of eighteen years, the juvenile court has jurisdiction to hear and dispose of such complaint, the same as if the complaint and hearing were held before such child arrived at the age of eighteen years, except that this section prohibits the commitment of such child to an Industrial School if, at the time of such hearing, it has arrived at the age of eighteen years.5

If the order of commitment is made before arriving at the age of eighteen years, such child must be accepted by an industrial school after arriving at the age of eighteen, if such commitment has not been terminated or suspended.6 9.

JUVENILE COURT (g) Exclusion From Hearings. 

There is some question as to whether or not the provision "in the hearing of any case the general public may be excluded and only such person admitted as have a direct interest in the case," is constitutional.

Art. I, Section 16 of the Constitution of Ohio provides that 

"all courts shall be open," which means open to the public, in the sense that the public has a right to attend.7 

Limited to Friends Perhaps

However, many courts have held that a judge in using his sound discretion may limit the number who may be admitted in any case to conform with the facilities afforded, and to secure decorum, others have held that the section is complied with if the parties, witnesses, attorneys, if any, and any friends whom the parties wish admitted, are permitted to witness the proceedings.8

It is the writer's opinion that in the hearing of children's cases, the court may bar from the hearing room small children, curiosity seekers, and all other persons who do not appear to assist the court, in protecting the best interests of the child. 

There is no appeal on a question of law and fact from an order of the juvenile court in disposing of neglected, dependent or delinquent children. The regularity of proceedings can be tested in habeas corpus proceedings.9

An appeal on a question of law may be taken from an order disposing of neglected, dependent or delinquent children.10

SEC. 1639-31 G. C. AGE LIMITATIONS. 

Repealed. 11

SEC. 1639-32 G. C. WAIVER OF JURISDICTION. 

In any case involving a delinquent child under the provisions of this chapter who has committed an act which would be a felony if committed by an adult, the judge after full investigation and after a mental and physical examination of such child has been made by the bureau of juvenile research, or by some other public or private agency, or by a person or persons, qualified to make such examination, may order that such child enter into a recognizance with good and sufficient surety subject to the approval of the judge, for his appearance before the court of common pleas at the next term thereof, for such disposition as the court of common pleas is authorized to make for a like act committed by an adult; or the judge may exercise the other powers conferred in this chapter in disposing of such case.12 

DISCUSSION: 

Provisions for transfer of jurisdiction to the criminal court, in the discretion of the juvenile court, as recommended by the standard code committee, exist in the laws of most states. Their inclusion serves to allay the apprehensions of some who would otherwise oppose the entire law, or insist that the age limit be made lower. 

With the discretionary power in the juvenile court it is not likely to be abused, and the court will rarely need to exercise it. The discretion is generally limited as to the minimum age of the children and the character of the offenses. Some states make the limit too low or omit it altogether. Others allow waiver only after adjudication of delinquency and only after a physical and mental examination of the child.

The constitutionality of waiver provisions has been upheld.13

The transfer of a case to the court of common pleas under the section is permissive and not mandatory.14 

No such certification should be made, however serious, unless it is for the child's best interests.15 11. 12. 13. 

121 O. L. H. 463, effective Jan. 1, 1946. 

Sec. 1639-32, G. C., 117 O. L., 520, effective Aug. 19, 1937. 

Mason vs. Holloway, 19 Ala. App., 234 State vs. Rutledge, 13 

S. W. (2nd), 1931. 

14. Leonard vs. Licker, 3 Ohio App., 377, 23 O. C. C. (N. S.), 442; 

1938 Opin Atty. Gen., No. 3439. 

15. In re Heist, 11 O. O. 537, 27 O. L. A. 1, 3 Ohio Sup. 259. 108

When a child who has committed an act which would be a felony if committed by an adult is bound over to the court of common pleas, the grand jury is empowered to return an indictment proper under the facts submitted.1 

If a juvenile court refuses to hear a case involving a minor who has committed felonious acts it amounts to transferring the case to the court of common pleas.2 

For other provisions relating to the disposition of a child who has committed felonious acts, see next preceding section.

For forms under this section, see last two forms under 

Sec. 1639-30 G. C. 

SEC. 1639-33 G. C. SELECTION OF CUSTODIAL AGENCY. 

In placing a child under any guardianship or custody other than that of its parent, the court shall, when practicable, select a person, or an institution or agency governed by persons of like religious faith as that of the parents of such child, or in case of a difference in the religious faith of the parents, then of the religious faith of the child, or if the religious faith of the child is not ascertained, then of either of the parents.3

DISCUSSION: 

This section follows the recommendation of the standard code committee. Most states have similar provisions, though they vary in some detail, some specifying the religion of the child (New York) and some that of the parents (Illinois, Minnesota).

SEC. 1639-34 G. C. SUPPORT OF COMMITTED CHIL- DREN; NON-RESIDENTS. 

When a child has been committed as provided by this chapter, the court may make an examination regarding the income of the parents or guardian or person charged with its support, and may then order such parent or guardian or person pay for the care, maintenance and education of such child, and for expenses involved in providing orthopedic, medical or surgical treatment or special care of such child. The court shall have power to enter up judgment for the money due and to enforce such judgment by execution as in the common pleas court. 

Provided, however, that whenever a child which has a legal settlement in another county comes within the jurisdiction of the court, the court may certify such case to the court of the county of legal settlement exercising the powers and jurisdiction conferred in this chapter for further proceedings, and such court shall thereafter proceed as if the original complaint had been filed in said court.

Any expenses incurred for the care, support, maintenance, education or medical or surgical treatment or special care of

Any expense ordered by the court for the care, maintenance and education of dependent, neglected or delinquent children, or for orthopedic, medical or surgical treatment or special care of such children under the provisions of this chapter, except such part thereof as may be paid by the state or federal government, shall be paid from the county treasury upon specifically itemized vouchers, certified to by the judge of the court. The court shall not be responsible for any expense resulting from the commitment of children to any home, county department, board, certified organization, or other institution, association, or agency, unless such expense has been authorized by the court at the time of commitment.4

DISCUSSION: 

The court must not be handicapped in protecting children and removing them from vicious surroundings when necessitated by reason of lack of resources for their support. Responsibility of county or other local officials under other laws is often insufficient to secure their cooperation in this matter. The Chancery Court of England acted to protect children only when a property right was involved. This section provides for the care of children where ordinary resources are not available. 

A child must be committed as provided in Sec. 1639-30 

G. C., before payment can be made for the care and support of such child.5

Money collected by a county welfare board for the support of children committed to it should be paid into the county treasury.6 The support of a child a ward of the court is chargeable to the county having jurisdiction over said chiid regardless of the changed residence of its parents.7

It was formerly held that the Department of Public Welfare had to accept a child committed to it by a juvenile court for return to its legal residence subject to available funds to carry on such activity.8

SEC. 1639-35 G. C. WHEN JURISDICTION OVER CHILD TERMINATES. 

When a child is committed to the boys' or girls' industrial school, or to the Ohio state reformatory, or to the permanent custody of the state department of public welfare, the division of social administration in said department, a county department, board or a certified organization, the order shall state that such commitment is permanent and the jurisdiction of the court in respect to the child so committed shall cease and terminate at the time of commitment; except that if the division or any county department, board or certified organization having such permanent custody make application to the court for the termination of such custody, the court upon such application, after due notice and hearing and for good cause shown, may terminate such custody at any time prior to the child becoming of age. The court shall make disposition of the matter in whatever manner will serve the best interests of the child. All other commitments made by the court shall be temporary and shall continue for such period as designated by the court in its order, or until terminated or modified by the court, or until a child attains the age of twenty-one years.1 

1. Sec. 1639-35 G. C., 121 O. L., 566, effective Jan. 1, 1946. 112

5. co 

DISCUSSION: 

Continuing jurisdiction, usually to the age of twentyone, over children who, while on probation or otherwise under its care, pass the age limit for the court's acquiring jurisdiction is provided for in most states. Working out the problem of a child in need of the court's intervention is usually not a short-time task; the hearing and adjudication mark not its end but its beginning. To complete with good results a case begun while the child was under eighteen, continued supervision or discipline after that age is often imperative; to begin new proceedings, criminal in nature, in another court would often be impracticable or disastrous. If the child, after becoming 18, commits a criminal offense, he may be prosecuted in a criminal court.

In the absence of a statutory provision in the juvenile court law, decisions conflict as to whether the juvenile court's jurisdiction continues after commitment of a child to an institution.2 It is intended under this section that the court's jurisdiction should terminate when a commitment is made to a public institution. Provisions that jurisdiction continues in such cases are included in the laws of Alabama, Arkansas, California, Florida, Georgia, New York, and Oklahoma. Under the Massachusetts and Virginia laws the court has continuing jurisdiction over children committed to private agencies, but not over those committed to the State Board of Pubiic Welfare. 

2. In re Knowack, 158 N. Y., 482; State vs. Mulertz, 60 Col., 468; 

In re Johnson, 36 Cal. App., 319. 3. 

1937 Opin. Atty. Gen., No. 891. 

4. 1939 Opin. Atty. Gen., No. 1322. 

1940 Opin. Atty. Gen., No. 2959; Conti vs. Shriner, 30 O. L. A. 193. 

1938 Opin. Atty. Gen., No. 3356. 

Where a commitment does not terminate as provided in this section the child remains a ward of the court until it arrives at the age of 21 regardless of the residence of the parents.3

A child committed to an institution or agency certified by the department of public welfare may be transferred to another approved agency.4 A child committed to the permanent care and custody of the state welfare department remains a ward of the state until it arrives at the age of twenty-one years, but a court may change a temporary order at any time after notice,5 or terminate its jurisdiction.6 

Where a child by reason of abandonment, neglect or dependency has been committed by the juvenile court to the permanent care of the county or district children's home or of any other accredited institution or agency caring for dependent children, including the state department of public welfare, or where such child has been 113

JUVENILE COURT 

by the parent or guardian voluntarily surrendered to such institution or agency, such juvenile court may, upon application and after notice and hearing and for good cause shown, terminate such custody at any time before the child reaches the age of twenty-one years.7

Crippled children can only be temporarily committed to the state department of public welfare for orthopedic surgery under the provisions of Sec. 1352-8, 10, G. C.8

Such child must be kept in readiness for return to its parents. Children committed to the Ohio State Reformatory can now be released by the authorities of said institution without an order of the juvenile judge. 

SEC. 1639-36 G. C. JUDGE MAY REQUIRE REPORT FROM INSTITUTION. 

At any time the judge may require from an association receiving or desiring so to receive children, such reports, information and statements as he deems proper and necessary. 

He may at any time require from an association or institution, reports, information or statements concerning any child or children committed to it by him, under the provisions of this chapter.9

SEC. 1639-37 G. C. PLACING OF CHILDREN BY ASSO- CIATIONS FROM OTHER STATES. 

No person, or association of another state, incorporated or otherwise, shall place a child in a family home within the boundaries of this state, either with or without indenture or for adoption, unless such person or association shall have furnished the state department of public welfare with such guaranty as it may require that no child having a contagious disease, deformity, feeble mind or vicious character, shall be brought into this state by such person or association or its agents, and that such person or association will promptly receive and remove from the state a child brought into the state by such person, or association or its agents, which shall become a public charge, within the period of five years thereafter.1

DISCUSSION: 

A foreign corporation must comply with the provisions of this section.2

This section applies to a mother of a child who leaves her home state and comes to Ohio to give birth to a child.3

PROCEDURE IN CASES OF ADULTS SEC. 1639-39 G. C. HOW CHARGE AGAINST ADULT FILED. 

In cases against an adult under the provisions of this chapter, any person may file with the clerk of the court exercising the powers and jurisdiction conferred in this chapter, an affidavit, setting forth briefly, in plain and ordinary language, the charges against the accused, and he shall be tried thereon, and in such prosecution an indictment by the grand jury or information by the prosecuting attorney shall not be required. The clerk shall issue a warrant for the arrest of the accused, who, when arrested, shall be taken before the judge and tried according to the provisions of this chapter. 

The affidavit may be amended at any time before or during the trial. The judge of the juvenile court in his discretion may bind over to the grand jury the defendant in any case against an adult under the provisions of this chapter, where the act complained of constitutes a felony.5 

DISCUSSION: 

This and the following sections deal with contributing to delinquency, other offenses against children, and nonsupport; they confer jurisdiction on the juvenile court, but safeguard the constitutional rights of defendants. (these cases being of a criminal nature) and make the additional provisions for suspension of sentence and probation which are so often useful in the protection of children.

In some states defendants cannot be prosecuted without presentment or indictment by a grand jury. In the 

District of Columbia, a similar provision was held unconstitutional, but the Fifth Amendment to the Federal

Constitution, with which this provision was held to conflict, does not apply to the states. In Florida and in 

Massachusetts there are decisions indicating that similar constitutional provisions would not invalidate these sections. In West Virginia, however, it has been held that for violation of a non-support law defendants could be tried only after presentment or indictment, under a constitutional provision exempting from this procedure only offenses which a justice of the peace had jurisdiction to try.

Privacy in the trial of certain cases of adults is provided for by statute or rule of court in some states. In 4. 

121 O. L. H. 463. Effective Jan. 1, 1946. 

5. Sec. 1639-39 G. C., 119 O. L. 731, effective September 5, 1941. 115

JUVENILE COURT 

Massachusetts the lower court must and superior courts may exclude the general public from the trial of cases involving sex offenses, where the alleged victim was a minor under 17. In Minnesota the courts may, and on the request of either party must, exclude the general public from the hearing of cases of illegitimacy. And although the Minnesota constitution requires that all criminal trials be public, certain courts have made rules providing for the exclusion of the general public from trials involving sex offenses, with the consent of the defendant. 

(Title) 

The State of Ohio,

It is not necessary that an information be filed by the prosecuting attorney before a prosecution can be instituted.1 While it was formerly prejudicial error to amend an affidavit before or during the trial,2 it can now be done under the authority of this section. The juvenile court now has the authority to bind a defendant over to the grand jury if the act complained of constitutes a felony. The jurisdiction of the juvenile court over the misdemeanors of adults against minors is not exclusive.3

SEC. 1639-40 G. C. COMPLAINT AND ARREST ORDERED AFTER HEARING. 

If it appear at the hearing of a child that any person has abused or has aided, induced, caused, encouraged, or contributed to the dependency, neglect or delinquency of a child, or acted in a way tending to cause delinquency in such child, or that a person charged by law with the care, support, education or maintenance of any child, has abandoned, failed, refused, or neglected to support or sufficiently contribute toward the support, education and maintenance of such child, the judge may order a complaint filed against such person and proceed to hear and dispose of the case as provided in this chapter.4

DISCUSSION: 

The procedure outlined by this section is not exclusive. 

Other methods may be resorted to.5

SEC. 1639-41 G. C. EXPENSE OF EXTRADITING FUGI- 

TIVE.

When a person charged with the violation of any of the provisions of this chapter has fled to another state, or territory, and the governor has issued a requisition for such person, the board of county commissioners shall pay from the general expense fund of the county to the agent designated in such requisition, all necessary expenses incurred in pursuing and returning such prisoner so charged.6

SEC. 1639-42 G. C. PROSECUTING ATTORNEY, DUTY OF. 

On the request of the judge exercising such jurisdiction, the prosecuting attorney of the county shall prosecute all adults charged with violating any of the provisions of this chapter.7

SEC. 1639-43 G. C. BAIL. 

The provisions of law relating to bail in criminal cases in the common pleas court shall apply to adults committed or held under the provisions of this chapter so far as they are applicable.8

DISCUSSION: 

Bail is covered under sections 13435-1 et seq., of the

General Code.

SEC. 1639-44 G. C. JURY TRIAL. 

Any adult arrested under the provisions of this chapter, at any time before hearing, may demand a trial by jury, or the judge upon his own motion may call a jury. The statutes relating to the drawing and impaneling of jurors in criminal cases in the court of common pleas, other than in capital cases, shall apply to such jury trial. The compensation of jurors and costs of the clerk and sheriff shall be taxed and paid as in criminal cases in the court of common pleas.9 

DISCUSSION: 

Jurors are entitled to a sum not to exceed $5.00 a day and 3 cents a mile each way, once a week, for their services under the provisions of Sec. 11419-43 G. C. The rate per day is fixed by the common pleas judges. For challenges see Sec. 11419-51 et seq., G. C.

The failure to demand a trial by jury probably constitutes a waiver thereof,10 but to eliminate any question relative thereto, a defendant should be asked if he desires a jury trial on his plea of "not guilty," and if he answers in the negative the entry on arraignment should show his waiver.11 A juvenile court may call a jury for a term of court.12 

9. Sec. 1639-44, G. C., 117 O. L., 520, effective Aug. 19, 1937.

Walter vs. State, 3 Ohio App. 97, 19 O. C. C. (N. S.) 452.

SEC. 1639-45 G. C. PENALTY FOR ABUSE, OR AIDING AND ABETTING DELINQUENCY. 

Judge.

Whoever abuses a child or aids, abets, induces, causes, encourages or contributes toward the dependency, neglect or delinquency, as herein defined, of a child or a ward of the court, or acts in a way tending to cause delinquency in such child, or who aids, abets, induces, causes or encourages a child or a ward of the court, committed to the custody of any person, department, public or private institution, to leave the custody of such person, department, public or private institution, without legal consent, shall be fined not less than five dollars, nor more than one thousand dollars or imprisoned not less than ten days nor more than one year, or both. Each day of such contribution to such dependency, neglect or delinquency, shall be deemed a separate offense.13 

13. Sec. 1639-45, G. C., 117 O. L., 520, effective Aug. 19, 1937. 126

2. 3. 4. 5. 1. 45678 6. 

DISCUSSION: 

(a) Necessary Allegations. In cases for contributing to the delinquency of a minor, the affidavit must allege and the state must prove that the minor is under eighteen years of age, is a delinquent within the meaning of the statute, and that the defendant is guilty of contributing to such delinquency.1 The affidavit should specify the acts of delinquency.2 The same details apply to other charges under this section except that a child need not be alleged to be delinquent if the charge is "acting in a way tending to cause delinquency." An affidavit need not be accompanied by an information signed by the prosecuting attorney.3 

(b) What Constitutes Aiding, Abetting, Etc., Delin quency. Conducting a house of ill repute to which a person was admitted,4 failure to provide schooling for a child,5 permitting a child to play pools and entering the marriage relation by swearing falsely to obtain a license7 are acts which are sufficient to sustain a conviction of contributing to the delinquency of a minor.

Where the owner of an establishment dispensing intoxicating liquors leaves the place and instructs her son, a minor, to "watch the bartender" while she is out on an errand, and during her absence the son sells intoxicating liquors to minors, such mother had acted "in a way tending to cause delinquency" of her son.8

A person who sells intoxicating liquors to a child under eighteen years "induces its delinquency" although the seller honestly believes it to be over that age.9 (c) Commitment. The imprisonment may be in either a county jail or workhouse,10 but not in a reformatory, except as provided in Sec. 1639-48 G. C.11 A fine must be collected during the lifetime of the defendant.12 

A sentence to one year in the Workhouse and a fine of $1000 and costs under this section is not affected by Sec.

JUVENILE COURT 

13451-9 G. C., as that section only applies to a fine,13 but the maximum imprisonment for non-payment of a fine is six months. (d) Evidence. In a prosecution for contributing to the delinquency of a child, the delinquency of the child must be proven before a conviction can be sustained. This can be accomplished in two ways: (1) By offering in evidence the judgment of the Court adjudging the child to be a delinquent.14 (2) By other proper evidence in the case. 15

The proper procedure in proving that there has been an adjudication of delinquency is to offer proof of the judgment of the Court which adjudicates the child to be a delinquent, and this requirement is satisfied where there was no offer of any adjudication of delinquency, if the child testified that he had been adjudged a delinquent without objection.16

It is not necessary to establish by a separate proceeding that the child was adjudged a delinquent. It is sufficient to establish by evidence, like any other necessary fact in a criminal case, that the child was guilty of acts of delinquency to which the accused contributed.1 

Evidence that the defendant and the mother of three small children lived at three different places in the same rooming house and accompanied each other on various trips is sufficient to support a conviction for contributing to the delinquency of such children.2 (e) Effect of Judgment. A conviction for contributing to the delinquency is not a bar to a prosecution for a felony involving the same circumstances, such as rape, for both are separate offenses.3 (f) Charge to Jury. If a charge of the court, taken in its entirety, is such that it is evident that the jury understood it in a manner so as to apply the law correctly, it will not be held erroneous, even though it contains some statements that are not strictly clear or proper.4

For a charge of contributing to the delinquency of a minor see 

Ferrell vs. Hornavius, 31 O. L. A. 460. 

(g) Mann Federal White Slave Law. Under the Mann 

Act, known as the Federal "White Slave" Law, any person who knowingly transports or causes to be transported, or aids or assists in obtaining transportation for, or in transporting any woman or girl over state lines, or persuades, induces, entices or coerces a woman or girl to go from one place to another over state lines, for the 13. 

In re Benedict, 142 O. S. 632, 53 N. E. (2d) 646.

Anss vs. State, 16 Ohio App. 502.

purposes of prostitution, debauchery, or for immoral purposes, is guilty of a felony and is subject to a fine not exceeding Five Thousand Dollars ($5000.00), or imprisonment of not more than five years, or both. 

If a girl is under eighteen, and subject to the Mann

Act, the penalty is a fine of not more than Ten Thousand 

Dollars ($10,000.00) or imprisonment not to exceed ten years, or both. 

Whoever keeps, maintains, controls, supports or harbors in any house or place for the purpose of prostitution, any alien woman or girl within three years after entry into the United States from a country which is a party to the agreement for the regulation and prevention of transportation in foreign commerce of alien women and girls for purposes of prostitution and debauchery, must notify the Commissioner of Immigration and Naturalization of the presence of such woman or girl within thirty days after such person commences to keep, maintain, control, support or harbor such woman or girl, and for failure to do so is subject to a fine of not more than Two 

Thousand Dollars ($2000.00) or imprisonment for a term not exceeding two years, or both.

Jurisdiction to try such cases is vested in the district federal court in which district such violation was committed, or from, through or into which any such woman or girl may have been carried or transported as a passenger. (h) Venue. Venue need not be proved in express terms, and is sufficiently shown by testimony of residents living near the scene of the crime in such county and by the arresting officer.1

In a prosecution for contributing to delinquency of minor children, based upon charges that the defendant lived with the mother of infant children while the husband and father was in military service, the venue is to be deemed sufficient proof although there is no direct testimony that the offense charged occurred in the county of the prosecution except as the same appears from the names of the streets where the parties lived together and the frequent allusions to incidents locating them in a city within the county.2 (i) New Trial. It is not error to overrule a motion for a new trial where such motion is supported by an affidavit of an officer of the State Liquor Control Department that he had the place of the defendant under surveillance and did not see any minor under eighteen years of age in or about the premises, as such evidence although newly discovered is entirely negative in nature.3

JUVENILE COURT 

The following is a charge to the jury under a prosecution for contributing to the delinquency of a minor: 

CHARGE TO JURY 

Contributing to Delinquency one.

The complaint charges in substance in this case, that on or about the ... did contribute towards the delinquency of one a minor under the age of 18 years, to-wit, of the age of years, in that said. on said day (here state charge). The complaint is no evidence of guilt and should not be considered by you as such.

This charge is based upon two sections of the statute, the one defining who is to be considered a delinquent child, providing in brief, so far as it relates to this case that a delinquent child includes any child under the age of 18 years who is incorrigible, or is guilty of immoral conduct, or violates a state law. (Or any other act of delinquency.) .....

The other section provides that whoever aids, abets, induces, causes, encourages, or contributes toward the delinquency of a minor under the age of eighteen years, or acts in a way tending to cause delinquency in such minor shall be punished as therein provided.

The juvenile laws were enacted to protect and correct delinquent children, and to punish those adults who may be responsible for said delinquency.

The Proof 

A defendant in a criminal case is presumed to be innocent and this presumption can only be overcome by proof which removes from your minds every reasonable doubt.

A presumption of innocence is an inference or taking for granted that a defendant is not guilty and before the defendant can be found guilty as he stands charged in the complaint, the State must prove beyond a reasonable doubt, the material allegations of the complaint, among which are that the female, is under the age of eighteen years, to-wit, about years of age; that she is a delinquent child; and that the defendant contributed toward her delinquency. ··9 ..

The statute fixes the age under which a child may be regarded as a delinquent, as 18 years, and the complaint alleges that is about.... years of age. You should therefore, determine first of all the age of said .. It is not necessary that the State prove her exact age. If you are satisfied, beyond a reasonable doubt, that she is under 18 years of age, and about the age stated in the complaint, that is sufficient. 

If you find that ... is under 18 years of age, you should next determine whether or not she is a delin- 130

quent child. If you find beyond a reasonable doubt that 

(here state acts of delinquency) Belief as to Girl's Age 

The record of the adjudication of ...... in this court under date of .. upon the charge of delinquency has been admitted in evidence solely for the purpose of tending to show that she is a delinquent child. But you are not to find the defendant guilty of the charge against him in this case on this record if the other elements of the offense alleged in the complaint are not proven beyond a reasonable doubt. 

If you should find that said. is under 18 years of age and that she is a delinquent child, you should then proceed to the consideration of the question whether the State has proved, beyond a reasonable doubt, that at the time and place named in the complaint contributed to the delinquency of said (here state charge) by ..9 

If you find beyond a reasonable doubt that child under 18 years of age, to-wit, about age; that she 

·9 • 9 is a years of (state facts back of complaint) then you would be justified in finding the defendant guilty, if in your judgment such acts of the said ... . constitute (state facts of delinquency)

It is claimed by the defendant that he believed the said .... was over 18 years of age. If an imposition was practiced on the defendant, so that the defendant had no intention to violate the law, then he must be deemed not guilty. But to fully rebut evidence of guilty knowledge as to the age of said the defendant should show that he made inquiry of persons well acquainted with said and was misled thereby.

His good faith and due care in seeking and obtaining such information, as well as the proper effect thereof under all the circumstances, are left to you to decide. 

If you are convinced beyond a reasonable doubt of the defendant's guilt, it is your duty to say so, and the jurors should not fail in their duty or return a verdict of not guilty, when in their own minds they are convinced, beyond a reasonable doubt, that the defendant is guilty, simply because of good-fellowship, kind heartedness, or a desire not to work a hardship on a fellow citizen. 

Disregard Suspicion

On the other hand, you are not to find the defendant guilty, unless the state has proved, beyond a reasonable doubt, all the elements of the offense charged. A mere suspicion of the defendant's guilt will not be sufficient, 131

JUVENILE COURT 

but you must be satisfied beyond a reasonable doubt of his guilt.

Reasonable Doubt 

The court has instructed you that all the material elements of the charge against the defendant must be proved beyond a reasonable doubt. A reasonable doubt is such a doubt as would exist in the mind of a reasonable man after a full, fair and careful examination and comparison of all the evidence. It is such a doubt as would cause a careful and prudent man to pause and consider before acting in the grave and important affairs of life. 

It is not a mere possible doubt, because everything relating to human affairs or depending on moral evidence is open to some possible or imaginary doubt. It is that state of the case which, after the entire comparison and consideration of all the evidence, leaves the minds of the jurors in that condition that they cannot say they feel an abiding conviction to a moral certainty of the truth of the charge. Nor is it a mere captious, ingenious, artificial doubt, formed in the mind of a juror in order to 

Escape the performance of a disagreeable duty. Nor does proof beyond a reasonable doubt mean absolute certainty-but a reasonable doubt, as the term is used in these instructions, must be one arising from a candid and impartial investigation of all the evidence. A doubt produced by an undue sensibility in the mind of a juror, in view of the consequences of his verdict is not a reasonable doubt and the jury is not allowed to create sources of materials of doubt by resorting to trivial or fanciful suppositions and remote conjectures not shown by the evidence. You are not at liberty to disbelieve as jurors, if from all the evidence you believe as men. Your oath imposes on you no obligation to doubt, where no doubt would exist if no oath had been administered. If, after a careful and impartial examination and consideration of all the evidence in the case, you can say that you feel an abiding conviction of the guilt of the defendant, and are fully satisfied to a moral certainty of the truth of the charge against him, then you are satisfied beyond a reasonable doubt. You will look then, to all the evidence, and if that satisfies you beyond a reasonable doubt of the defendant's guilt, you must find that to be the fact by your verdict. If you are not so fully satisfied, but find that there are only strong probabilities of guilt, your only safe course is to acquit.

Jury Decides Facts 

You are the sole judges of the facts in this case and of the weight and credibility to be given to the testimony of the different witnesses. The court is the sole judge of the law, but it is the duty of the jury to find and determine the facts and apply thereto the law as given by 132

the court. In determining the guilt or innocence of the defendant in this case you must not consider the question of punishment as that is a function which rests solely with the judge in case the defendant is found guilty. 

In determining the credit you will give a witness and the weight and value you will attach to his testimony, you should consider his conduct and appearance before you; his interest, if any, in the result of the case; his motives; his bias or prejudice, if any has been disclosed; the opportunity the witness had to be informed or to observe as to the matters testified to by him; his intelligence; his inclination and disposition to tell the truth or otherwise, and the probability or improbability of his statements taken together in connection with the other facts and circumstances shown in evidence. Taking all these matters into account, it is your duty to give to each of the witnesses such credit, and to the testimony of each witness such value as you may think proper.

While the State must prove its case beyond a reasonable doubt, still such proof need not be entirely direct evidence of persons who saw the occurrence sought to be proved, but facts may also be proved by circumstantial evidence, that is, by proof of circumstances, if any, such as give rise to a reasonable inference in the minds of the jury of the truth of the facts alleged and sought to be proved, provided such circumstances, together with all the evidence in the case, prove the defendant's guilt beyond a reasonable doubt.

If there is real or apparent conflict in the evidence, it is your duty to reconcile that conflict as far as possible. 

It is your duty to determine what you will accept as true, and what you will reject as false. 

Unworthy Witness

Evidence has been introduced here for the purpose of impeaching a witness. A witness is successfully impeached, when the evidence satisfies the jury that the witness is not worthy of credit. Whether a witness has been impeached is a matter for the jury. If the jury believes the evidence, offered for the purpose of impeaching a witness, shows that a witness is not worthy of belief, in the opinion of the jury, then the jury would set aside his testimony and would not consider it further, unless it was corroborated by other evidence or circumstances in the case. After all the credibility of a witness is a matter peculiarly for the jury even though evidence may be introduced for the purpose of impeaching a witness.

The defendant is a competent witness in his own behalf, and his testimony is to be received by you and weighed by the same rules as the testimony of other witnesses. In passing upon what weight you will give to his testimony, you may take into consideration the fact 133

JUVENILE COURT 

that he is the defendant, and his interest in the result of the trial.

Testimony Controls 

You must decide this case upon the testimony which has been given by the witnesses. Statements made by counsel in arguments to the court upon the evidence or arguments to you, as to what the evidence is, are not to be considered as evidence by you, unless it corresponds with your memory of the evidence as you heard the witnesses give it.

Two forms of verdict will be given you. If under the evidence and charge of the court, you find the defendant guilty as he stands charged in the complaint, you will use the form of verdict marked No. 1. If under said evidence and instructions you find the defendant is not guilty, you will use the form of verdict maked No. 2.

In criminal cases the verdict of the jury must be unanimous, and signed by one of your number as foreman, whom you will select when you retire to the jury room. 

SEC. 1639-46 G. C. FAILURE OR NEGLECT TO SUPPORT; 

PENALTY. 

Whoever is charged by law with the care, support, maintenance or education of a legitimate or illegitimate child under eighteen years of age, and fails, neglects or refuses so to do, or who abandons such child, or who beats, neglects, injures or otherwise ill-treats such child, or causes or allows him or her to engage in common begging and whoever is charged by law with the care, support, maintenance or education of a legitimate or illegitimate child under twenty-one years of age who is physically or mentally handicapped, and fails, neglects or refuses to care for, support, maintain or educate such child, upon complaint filed in a court exercising the jurisdiction conferred in this chapter, may be, after trial and conviction, sentenced to imprisonment for not more than one year, or fined not more than five hundred dollars, or both, and the judge may order that such person stand committed until such fines and costs are paid; provided, that if he shall pay promptly each week to the court or to a trustee named by such court a sum to be fixed by it for such purpose, sentence may be suspended. Such neglect, non-support or abandonment shall be deemed to have been committed in the county in which such child may be at the time of such neglect, nonsupport or abandonment. Each dav of such failure, neglect, or refusal shall constitute a separate offense.4

SEC. 1639-47 G. C. WORKHOUSE SENTENCE; PROVI- SION FOR DEPENDENT CHILDREN; DISBURSING OFFICER. 

When an adult is convicted and sentenced to imprisonment in a jail or workhouse for any violation of the provisions of the next preceding section, the county from which such person is so sentenced, on the order of the judge, shall pay from the general revenue fund fifty cents, for each day such prisoner is confined, to the juvenile court of such county, for the maintenance of the dependent children of such prisoner. Such expenditures shall be made under the direction of the judge, who shall designate an employee for such purpose. 

The county commissioners of such county shall make an appropriation for such cases, and allowances therefrom shall be paid from the county treasury upon the warrant of the county auditor.3

SEC. 1639-48 G. C. COMMITMENTS TO WOMEN'S RE- FORMATORY. 

When any female over the age of eighteen years is found guilty of a misdemeanor under the provisions of this chapter, the judge may order such female confined to the women's reformatory at Marysville for the same term for which said female could be committed to a workhouse or jail.4

This section is constitutional.5

Where a criminal statute authorizes as a penalty for its violation the imposition of a sentence to imprisonment for "not less than ten days nor more than one year," a sentence for "not to exceed one year," though subject to reversal for indefiniteness, is not void, and it is the duty of the authorities of the institution to which the person so sentenced is committed to retain custody of the prisoner for the full period of one year unless he is sooner released by order of a court of competent jurisdiction. Where a female over the age of eighteen years if found guilty by a juvenile court on seven separate counts, of contributing to the delinquency of children, and is sentenced pursuant to Sec. 1639-48 G. C., to the 

Ohio Reformatory for Women, such sentences by their terms being for a term "not to exceed one year" and the court has not specified that such sentences are to run concurrently, it is the duty of the officers of such reformatory to hold such prisoner for the full period of seven years, unless she is sooner released from custody by order of a court having jurisdiction to order such release. The provisions of Sec. 13457-1, G. C., relating to increased sentences for second and third offenders in certain misdemeanor cases, apply to commitments made to the Ohio Reformatory for Women, pursuant to Sec.

SEC. 1639-49 G. C. SUSPENSION OF SENTENCE. 

In every case of conviction and where imprisonment is imposed as part of the punishment, such judge may suspend sentence, before or during commitment, upon such condition as he imposes.7

DISCUSSION: 

The court has jurisdiction over such suspension as long as it has jurisdiction over the child involved. Such suspensions are not governed by the criminal code.8 It may suspend sentence before or after commitment, but may not postpone the imposition of sentence.9

SEC. 1639-50. FORFEIT OF BOND. 

When, as a condition of suspension of sentence, bond is required and given, upon the failure of a person giving such bond to comply with the terms and conditions thereof, such bond may be forfeited, the suspension terminated by the judge, the original sentence executed as though it had not been suspended, and the term of any sentence imposed in such case shall commence from the date of imprisonment of such person after such forfeiture and termination of suspension.

Any part of such sentence which may have been served, shall be deducted from any such period of imprisonment. When such bond is forfeited the judge may issue execution thereon without further proceedings.10

SEC. 1639-51. APPEAL. 

The provisions of the law relating to appeals on questions of law from the court of common pleas, including the allowance and signing of bills of exceptions shall apply to prosecutions of adults under this chapter, and from such prosecutions an appeal on a question of law may be taken to the court of appeals of the county under laws governing appeals in other 

10. Sec. 1639-50 G. C., 117 O. L., 520, effective Aug. 19, 1937. 139

JUVENILE COURT 

criminal cases to such court of appeals. An appeal shall not be taken to the court of appeals except upon good cause shown, upon motion and notice to the prosecuting attorney, as in civil cases, or unless such motion is allowed by such court.11

DISCUSSION: 

(a) Adults. For procedure, see Sec. 12223-1 G. C., et seq. (b) Children. There is no specific provision governing appeals in children's cases and such appeals are governed by the appellate procedure act, Sec. 12223-1 et seq., G. C.1

An appeal on a question of law may be prosecuted from an order declaring a child to be neglected, dependent or delinquent.2 A judgment making a child dependent, and awarding custody "until further order of the court" is a final order from which an appeal on a question of law may be taken.3 

Where an adult father files a motion to vacate the previous orders and to dismiss proceedings of a Juvenile Court, the one order, in part, adjudicating that his child is a dependent child and the other awarding its exclusive custody to the mother, and at the hearing on such motion it was stipulated that the sole issue was the court's jurisdiction to hear, decide and make the order delivered therein on a stated date, there is no right of appeal to the Court of Appeals on questions of law from the court's order overruling such motion, such proceedings not being classified as a prosecution of an adult within the meaning of the Juvenile Court Code.4

A judgment finding that defendant is a delinquent child and committing her to an industrial school may not be appealed to the Court of Appeals upon questions of law and fact since the action is not one cognizable in chancery at common law, and where an order committing a child to an institution is made by the juvenile court, the refusal of such court to allow a bail bond pending appeal is not prejudicial.5 A finding of dependency is not a decree in chancery and therefore is not appealable on law and fact.6

A bastardy proceeding, while having some characteristics of a criminal prosecution, is essentially a civil action and an appeal from a judgment therein is not controlled by the provisions of Section 1639-51, General Code, re-

GENERAL PROVISIONS SEC. 1639-52 G. C. SERVICE OF WRITS. 

The summons, warrants, citations, subpoenas and other writs of such court may issue to a probation officer of any such court or to the sheriff of any county or any marshal, constable or police officer, and the provisions of law relating to the subpoenaing of witnesses in other cases shall apply in so far as they are applicable.8

DISCUSSION 

A probation officer cannot be held liable for serving a writ insufficient in law if it is prima facie regular.9 

SEC. 1639-53 G. C. EXPENSES OF SERVING WRITS. 

When a summons, warrant, citation, subpoena or other writ is issued to any such officer, other than a probation officer, the expense in serving the same shall be paid by the county, township or municipality in the manner prescribed by law for the payment of sheriffs, deputies, assistants and other employees.1

SEC. 1639-54 G. C. EXAMINATION BY COMPETENT PHYSICIAN. 

Any person coming within the provision of this chapter may be subjected to a physical and mental examination by a competent physician or physicians, psychologists and/or psychiatrists, to be appointed by the juvenile court. Whenever any child is committed to any institution by virtue of the provision of this chapter, a record of such examination or examinations shall be sent with the commitment to such institution. The compensation of such physicians, psychologists and/or psychiatrists and the expenses of such examinations shall be paid by the county treasurer upon specifically itemized vouchers, certified by the judge of the court.2

It is hereby made the duty of every county, township or municipal official or department including the prosecuting attorney to render all assistance and cooperation within his
